A/K_{w}
has good reduction, its Néron model
\mathscr{A}/\mathcal{O}_{K_{w}}
is an abelian scheme. It satisfies the Néron mapping property
\mathscr{A}(\mathcal{O}_{K_{w}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}A(K_{w})
. Since
\mathscr{A}
is an abelian scheme,
\mathscr{A}[p^{n}]
is a finite flat group scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{K_{w}}
for all
n\geq 1
. Now for any finite flat group scheme
G
over
\mathcal{O}_{K_{n,w}}
, one has an exact sequence

0=G^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{K_{n,w}})\to G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{n,w}})\to G^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{O}_{K_{n,w}})

coming from the connected-étale sequence. Since
\mathcal{O}_{K_{n,w}}
is a Henselian local ring,
G^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{O}_{K_{n,w}})\simeq G^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathbf{F}_{n})
for the finite residue field
\mathbf{F}_{n}
of
\mathcal{O}_{K_{n,w}}
. Note that since
K_{\infty,w}/K_{w}
is infinitely ramified, the
G^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{O}_{K_{n,w}})
will eventually stabilize for
n\to\infty
if
G=\mathscr{A}[p^{n}]
since an abelian variety has only finitely many points over a finite field. Hence
A[p^{\infty}](K_{\infty,w})=\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})
is finite.

For future use, we mention that the same argument gives the finiteness of
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},{\operatorname{Fil}}^{-}(M_{f}))
if one replaces
G
by
G^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}
throughout. ∎

Lemma 1.3.5
.

Let
w\mid p
in
K
. Then
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},M_{f})
has
\Lambda
-corank
2
.

Proof.

Let
X\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},M_{f})^{\vee}
. Consider the exact sequences

(1.11)

0\to V_{f}/T_{f}\to M_{f}\xrightarrow{\text{$\cdot T$ }}M_{f}\to 0

and

(1.12)

0\to\overline{\rho}_{f}\to V_{f}/T_{f}\xrightarrow{\text{$\cdot\pi$ }}V_{f}/T_{f}\to 0.

We aim to study the
\mathcal{O}
-rank of
X/T
and mimic our argument in
proposition

1.1.3
(ii). We will show that
X[T]=0
and the
\mathcal{O}
-rank is
2
, then the
\Lambda
-rank of
X
is
2
by the topological Nakayama lemma as in
lemma

1.1.2
.

We would like to study the following short exact sequence coming from (
1.11
)

(1.13)

0\to\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})/T\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})\to X^{\vee}[T]\to 0,

where the first term is finite by
proposition

1.3.4
(i).

By (
1.12
), there is an exact sequence

(1.14)

0\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})/\mathfrak{p}\to\text{{H}}^{2}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f})\to\text{{H}}^{2}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]\to 0.

We claim that the last term in the above sequence is
0
. Indeed,
\text{{H}}^{2}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})=0
because it’s dual to
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},T_{f})
which must vanish because
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})
is finite by
proposition

1.3.4
(ii).
Note that there is a sequence

0\to\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},\mathbf{F}(\omega))=0\to\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f})\to\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{1}))=\mathbf{F},

so
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f})
has
\mathbf{F}
-dimension
0
or
1
. From local duality and self-duality of
\overline{\rho}_{f}
,
\text{{H}}^{2}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f})=\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f})^{\vee}
has the same dimension. Now we consider two cases.

Case I:

{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}(\text{{H}}^{2}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f}))={\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}(\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f}))=0
.

In this case, from the local Euler characteristic formula
[
NSW08
, Theorem 7.3.1]
,
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f})
has
\mathbf{F}
-dimension
2
. By (
1.12
), there is an identification

(1.15)

\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]\simeq\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f})/(\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})/\mathfrak{p}).

Since in this case
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})=0
, from (
1.15
) we see that
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]
has
\mathbf{F}
-dimension
2
. Therefore
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})/\mathfrak{p}=0
, so
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})
is
\mathcal{O}
-cofree of rank
2
. Hence
X/T
is also
\mathcal{O}
-free of rank
2
.

Case II:

{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}(\text{{H}}^{2}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f}))={\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}(\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f}))=1
.

From (
1.14
), we see that
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})/\mathfrak{p}
also has
\mathbf{F}
-dimension
1
.
Again by the local Euler characteristic formula,
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},\overline{\rho}_{f})
has
\mathbf{F}
-dimension
4
. Since in this case
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})
is non-trivial, we see from
proposition

1.3.4
(ii) that
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})/\mathfrak{p}=\mathbf{F}
. So by (
1.15
),
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]
has
\mathbf{F}
-dimension
3
. Hence
{\operatorname{rk}}_{\mathcal{O}}(X/T)={\operatorname{corank}}_{\mathcal{O}}(X^{\vee}[T])={\operatorname{corank}}_{\mathcal{O}}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})=2
.

Now we show that
X[T]=0
. Indeed, the same arguments in
proposition

1.1.3
Claim 0 shows
\text{{H}}^{2}(K_{w},M_{f})=0
. We therefore have an identification
X^{\vee}/T=\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},M_{f})/T\cong\text{{H}}^{2}(K_{w},V_{f}/T_{f})=0
. Now by
lemma

1.1.2
((i) for Case I and (ii) for Case II),
X
has
\Lambda
-rank
2
.
∎

Similarly as in
proposition

1.1.3
, we define the restriction map for
M_{f}
by

{\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}}\colon\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},M_{f})\to\text{{H}}^{1}(I_{w},M_{f})^{G_{w}/I_{w}}.

Define
{\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]}
by replacing
M_{f}
with
M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]
in the above definition.

Lemma 1.3.6
.

\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})
is finite and cyclic. Moreover, when
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})
is non-trivial,
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})=\mathbf{F}
and when
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})=0
,
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})=0
.

Proof.

Similar to (
1.2
), there are two exact sequences for
M_{f}
and
M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]
respectively:

(1.16)

0\to\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})^{\vee}\to(M_{f}^{\vee})_{I_{w}}\to(M_{f}^{\vee})_{I_{w}}\to M_{f}^{\vee}/T\to 0.

and

(1.17)

0\to\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})^{\vee}\to(M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]^{\vee})_{I_{w}}\to(M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]^{\vee})_{I_{w}}\to M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]^{\vee}/T\to 0.

Note that the last term in (
1.16
) is nothing but the dual of
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})
, which is finite by
proposition

1.3.4
(iii). Therefore
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})^{\vee}=\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})=\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}^{n}
for some
n\geq 0
.

Note that
(M_{f}^{\vee})_{I_{w}}
is
\Lambda
-torsion because
I_{w}
is non-trivial by
lemma

1.0.2
(iii), so
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})
is also finite because characteristic ideals are multiplicative in exact sequences of torsion
\Lambda
-modules. Moreover, if
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})=0
, by Noetherian property of
\Lambda
the
\Lambda
-module surjection

(M_{f}^{\vee})_{I_{w}}\to(M_{f}^{\vee})_{I_{w}}

must be an isomorphism so
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})=0
. Before proving it’s always cyclic, we first prove the second half of this proposition.

First assume
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})
is non-trivial and hence cyclic, then
M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]^{\vee}/T=\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])^{\vee}=(\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}])^{\vee}=\mathbf{F}
. Since
(M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]^{\vee})_{I_{w}}=(\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]))^{\vee}=(\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w,\infty},\overline{\rho}_{f}))^{\vee}
is finite (the second equality follows from Shapiro’s lemma), it follows from sequence (
1.17
) that
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})=\mathbf{F}
.

Now if
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})=0
, then
M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]^{\vee}/T=0
so again from sequence (
1.17
) we see that
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})=0
.

Finally, we show
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})[\mathfrak{p}]\subseteq\mathbf{F}
, from which it follows immediate that
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})
is cyclic because we have showed it’s finite.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker({\operatorname{res}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(I_{w},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(I_{w},M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}({\operatorname{res}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{res}}}

When
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})=0
, we have seen that
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})=0
.

Now assume
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})
is non-trivial so
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}=\mathbf{F}
. In this case also
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})=\mathbf{F}
. Since again
\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]=\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])=\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w,\infty},\overline{\rho}_{f})
, we have an exact sequence

0\to\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w,\infty},\mathbf{F}(\omega))=0\to\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])\to\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w,\infty},\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{1}))=\mathbf{F}.

Here
\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w,\infty},\mathbf{F}(\omega))=0
because
\omega
is ramified and
I_{w}/I_{w,\infty}
is a pro-
p
group while the trivializing extension of
\omega
has order dividing
p-1
. Hence
\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]\subseteq\mathbf{F}
. It follows that
\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f})\subseteq F/\mathcal{O}
and
\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}\subseteq\mathbf{F}
.

Now if
\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}=0
, from the above diagram we see that
{\operatorname{coker}}({\operatorname{res}})=0
and the snake lemma yields a surjection

\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})=\mathbf{F}\to\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})[\mathfrak{p}],

so
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})\subseteq\mathbf{F}
.

If
\text{{H}}^{0}(I_{w},M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}=\mathbf{F}
, then
\ker({\operatorname{res}})={\operatorname{coker}}({\operatorname{res}})\subseteq\mathbf{F}
. If both are
0
,
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})[\mathfrak{p}]=\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})=\mathbf{F}
. If both are
\mathbf{F}
, then the snake lemma yields an injection

\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})[\mathfrak{p}]\to{\operatorname{coker}}({\operatorname{res}})=\mathbf{F},

so again
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})[\mathfrak{p}]\subseteq\mathbf{F}
.
∎

1.4.
Selmer groups of
\rho_{f}

Similarly as for that of
M_{\vartheta}
, we define an
unramified Selmer group
for
f
by

\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,M_{f})=\ker\Big\{\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{res}}_{{\operatorname{nr}},f}}\prod_{w\in\Sigma,w\nmid p}\text{{H}}^{1}(I_{w},M_{f})^{G_{w}/I_{w}}\times\text{{H}}^{1}(I_{\overline{v}},M_{f})^{G_{\overline{v}}/I_{\overline{v}}}\Big\},

and an
S
-imprimitive unramified Selmer group
, where
S=\Sigma-\{v,\overline{v},\infty\}
, by

\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}^{S}}(K,M_{f})=\ker\Big\{\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{res}}^{S}_{{\operatorname{nr}},f}}\text{{H}}^{1}(I_{\overline{v}},M_{f})^{G_{\overline{v}}/I_{\overline{v}}}\Big\}.

The residual Selmer groups
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])
and

\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}^{S}}(K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])
are defined in the same manner.

One can similarly define the Greenberg Selmer groups for
f
by imposing triviality at
\overline{v}
.

By Ribet’s Lemma
[
Bel09
]
, from the decomposition
\overline{\rho}_{f}^{\operatorname{ss}}\cong\mathbf{F}(\varphi)\oplus\mathbf{F}(\psi)
one has an exact sequence

0\to\mathbf{F}(\varphi)\to\overline{\rho}_{f}\to\mathbf{F}(\psi)\to 0,

which further induces and exact sequence of
{\operatorname{Gal}}(K^{\Sigma}/K)
-modules

0\to M_{\varphi}[\mathfrak{p}]\to M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]\to M_{\psi}[\mathfrak{p}]\to 0,

where
\varphi\cdot\psi=\omega
. The cases left out in
[
CGLS22
]
are when one of
\varphi|_{G_{p}},\psi|_{G_{p}}
is trivial. Hence we assume this is the case.

Note that by Perrin-Riou’s formula in
[
KO20
, Proposition 2.9]
, the order of
\varphi
and
\psi
does not matter: Conjecture 1.2 (1) in
op. cit.
is true (the Selmer group is
\Lambda
-cotorsion), so Conjecture 1.2 (2) (the anticyclotomic Main Conjecture for our modular forms)
1
1

1

see also
remark

2.2.4
.
is independent of the lattice
T_{f}
. Therefore we are free to assume that
\varphi|_{G_{p}}=\omega
and
\psi|_{G_{p}}=\mathbf{1}
. We then write the global character
\varphi
(resp.
\psi
) as
\tilde{\omega}
(resp.
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}
). We remark here that the BSD Conjecture is also independent of the lattice (see for example
[
Mil06
, I.7]
).

From now on, by essentially choosing a lattice
T_{f}
, we fix the following ordering of the characters appearing in the above sequence

(1.18)

0\to M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}]\to M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]\to M_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}[\mathfrak{p}]\to 0.

We remark here that the second character
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}
may be non-trivial or trivial on
G_{K}
. Therefore,
\text{{H}}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}[\mathfrak{p}])
may or may not be
0
. Since the semisimplification
\overline{\rho}_{f}^{\operatorname{ss}}
is independent from the choice of a lattice
T_{f}
, we must study both cases. However, from
[
BP19
, Theorem 5.2]
, we can always choose a non-split extension of
\overline{\rho}_{f}^{\operatorname{ss}}
so that
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{1})
(the global trivial character) is never a subrepresentation, so we could always assume that
\text{{H}}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})=0
(equivalently,
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,\overline{\rho}_{f})=0
), i.e., there is no global torsion for
f
(it should be mentioned that their assumption (Good Eisen) is not necessary for our use). We will do so in the rest of this paper. We also include some partial results without assuming global non-torsionness in the appendix for interested readers.

Let
\mathfrak{X}_{f}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{f}^{S}
denote Pontryagin dual of the primitive and
S
-imprimitive unramified Selmer groups of
f
.
This section is devoted to proving the following relation between the
\lambda
-invariants of the imprimitive Selmer groups under the assumption that there is no global torsion:

Theorem 1.4.1
.

If
\varphi|_{G_{p}}=\omega
(so
\psi|_{G_{p}}=\mathbf{1}
) and
\varphi|_{G_{K}}\neq\omega
(so
\psi|_{G_{K}}\neq\mathbf{1}
). Then

\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f})=\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{\varphi})+\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{\psi}).

If
\varphi|_{G_{K}}=\omega
and
\psi|_{G_{K}}=\mathbf{1}
,

\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f})+1=\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{\varphi})+\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{\psi}).

Furthermore,
\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{f}
are both
\Lambda
-torsion with
\mu
-invariants
0
.

Proof of the theorem:

First notice that the sequence (
1.18
) yields a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{Gr}}^{S}}(K,M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{Gr}}^{S}}(K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{Gr}}^{S}}(K,M_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}[\mathfrak{p}])}

with exact rows. It then follows from
proposition

1.2.5
that
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{Gr}}^{S}}(K,M_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}[\mathfrak{p}])
and
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{Gr}}^{S}}(K,M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])
are finite (since Greenberg’s Selmer groups are smaller than unramified ones). Now
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{Gr}}^{S}}(K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])
is finite and there is an exact sequence

0\to\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{Gr}}^{S}}(K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])\to\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ur}\,}^{S}}(K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])\to\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})(\subset\mathbf{F}),

from which it follows that
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ur}\,}^{S}}(K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])
is finite.

We have the following 2 diagrams for
f
with exact rows and columns similar to those of the characters in Section
1.2
and
lemma

1.2.4
:

(Gr,
f
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(r_{\overline{v}}^{{\operatorname{Gr}},0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{Gr}}^{S}}(K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{Gr}}^{S}}(K,M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\overline{v}}^{{\operatorname{Gr}},0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\overline{v}}^{{\operatorname{Gr}},1}}

(nr,
f
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(r_{\overline{v}}^{{\operatorname{nr}},0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}^{S}}(K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}^{S}}(K,M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f})}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})}[\mathfrak{p}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\overline{v}}^{{\operatorname{nr}},0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\overline{v}}^{{\operatorname{nr}},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Since
\text{{H}}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})=0
and as we shall see,
\ker(f)\subseteq\mathbf{F}
,
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}^{S}}(K,M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]
must also be finite. Then from the structure theorem it follows that
\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f}
(and hence
\mathfrak{X}_{f}
) are
\Lambda
-torsion with
\mu
-invariants
0
. This proves the last part of the theorem.

Remark 1.4.2
.

As in
remark

1.2.3
, the global-to-local map defining the Selmer groups (both the Greenberg’s one and the unramified one, both primitive and imprimitive) for
f
is also surjective in our case, due to
[
PW11
, Proposition A.2]
.

(1) is already proved.

(2) is a proved above (both primitive and imprimitive).

To see (3), since
\rho_{f}
is self-dual, the cohomology group we consider is identified with
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty},V_{f}/T_{f})
. By the comments before
[
CGLS22
, Lemma 3.3.3]
, we have
\rho_{f}(G_{K_{\infty}})=\rho_{f}(G_{\mathbf{Q}})
, so we need to show that
\text{{H}}^{0}(\mathbf{Q},V_{f}/T_{f})
is finite. Assume this is infinite, then
V_{f}/T_{f}\cong(F/\mathcal{O})^{2}
has at least a
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
-stable copy of
F/\mathcal{O}
. Since we could recover
T_{f}=\varprojlim V_{f}/T_{f}[p^{n}]
from
V_{f}/T_{f}
, this implies
T_{f}
has a
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
-stable copy of
\mathcal{O}
. However, this contradicts the irreducibility of
T_{f}
as a
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
module, a result of Ribet in
[
Rib77
]
.

(4) holds by
lemma

1.3.5
(for Greenberg’s Selmer group) and
lemma

1.3.6
(for the unramified Selmer group).

To finish the proof of the theorem, it remains to compare the
\lambda
-invariants. Recall that by
lemma

1.3.6
,
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})
is finite. We now have
3
different cases to consider:

I

\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})
is non-trivial and
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})=0

II

Both
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})
and
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})
are non-trivial

III

\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},M_{f})=0
(so also
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})=\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})=0
)

For simplicity, we first assume
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})=0
. In this case, the map
f
is surjective.

Case I:

Note that
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f})
is cyclic and non-trivial, so by
proposition

1.3.4
(iii) one has
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}=\mathbf{F}
. Therefore
\ker(f)=0
. Also
\text{{H}}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})/\mathfrak{p}=0
by
proposition

1.3.3
.

To apply snake lemma, we add a fourth row to the unramified diagrams and abbreviate them as follows (here
?=\tilde{\mathbf{1}},f
or
\tilde{\omega}
):

(nr,
?
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{?})/\mathfrak{p}=0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{?}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{?})[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(f_{?})=0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{?}[\mathfrak{p}])}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{?}[\mathfrak{p}]})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{?})}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{?}})}[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}_{?[\mathfrak{p}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}_{?}[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{?}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{?[\mathfrak{p}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{?}\text{[$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{p}$]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{?}}

Here dashed lines are used because in the case
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})\neq 0
they may not be exact. We also remark that the cokernels appearing in the above diagrams are finite since all groups have finite
\mathcal{O}
-rank.

Note that by the structure theorem of finitely generated
\Lambda
-modules (since
\mu({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?}))=0
) the
\lambda
-invariants of
{\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})
can be computed as

\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{?})={\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}{\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})[\mathfrak{p}]-{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}{\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})/\mathfrak{p},

whereas
{\operatorname{coker}}_{?}[\mathfrak{p}]={\operatorname{im}}({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})[\mathfrak{p}]\to{\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})/\mathfrak{p})
by
remark

1.2.3
and
remark

1.4.2
, so in particular

\displaystyle{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}{\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})/\mathfrak{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}\ker({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})/\mathfrak{p}\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{?})/\mathfrak{p})+{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}{\operatorname{im}}\big({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})/\mathfrak{p}\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{?})/\mathfrak{p}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}{\operatorname{coker}}_{?}[\mathfrak{p}]+{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}\ker\Big(\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{?})/\mathfrak{p}\to\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{?})}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{?}})}/\mathfrak{p}\Big).

Denoting the last kernel by
K_{?}
, we get

\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{?})={\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}{\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{?})[\mathfrak{p}]-{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}{\operatorname{coker}}_{?}[\mathfrak{p}]-{\operatorname{dim}}_{\mathbf{F}}K_{?}.

Now by
corollary

1.2.6
, the last two terms are
0
when
?=\tilde{\mathbf{1}}
.
Hence, if we use
d(\cdot)
to represent the
\mathbf{F}
-dimension of an
\mathbf{F}
-vector space, we get the following

Lemma 1.4.3
.

We have

(i)

d({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}[\mathfrak{p}]))=\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}),

(ii)

d({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]))=\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f})+d({\operatorname{coker}}_{f[\mathfrak{p}]})+d(K_{f}),

(iii)

d({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}]))=\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{\tilde{\omega}})+d({\operatorname{coker}}_{\tilde{\omega}[\mathfrak{p}]})+d(K_{\tilde{\omega}}).

The next step is to relate the left hand sides of the above equations. Unlike in
[
CGLS22
]
, they do not simply satisfy an additive relation. Instead, we have

Lemma 1.4.4
.

If
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}\neq\mathbf{1}
(i.e., the local trivial character is not globally trivial), we have

d({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]))=d({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}]))+d(K)+d({\operatorname{coker}}_{f[\mathfrak{p}]})-d({\operatorname{coker}}_{\tilde{\omega}[\mathfrak{p}]})-d(C)+1,

where
K
(resp.
C
) is the kernel (resp. cokernel) of the map

{\operatorname{coker}}(\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]))\to{\operatorname{coker}}\Big(\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}]})}\to\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})}\Big).

If
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}=\mathbf{1}
, we have

d({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]))=d({\operatorname{Sel}}(M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}]))+d(K)+d({\operatorname{coker}}_{f[\mathfrak{p}]})-d({\operatorname{coker}}_{\tilde{\omega}[\mathfrak{p}]})-d(C).

Proof.

This is just generalized snake lemma:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0(}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}]})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}(g_{\overline{v}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}_{\tilde{\omega}[\mathfrak{p}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}_{f[\mathfrak{p}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{\overline{v}}}

Here the map
g
is injective if
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}\neq\mathbf{1}
and it has kernel
\mathbf{F}
if
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}=\mathbf{1}
.
2
2

2

This is the only place where global trivialness of the character makes a difference on the algebraic side. Similar difference also appears if we choose the other ordering of the characters, since it determines whether
f
has global torsion, as the (local) trivial character is a subrepresentation in that case. This difference corresponds to a different Iwasawa Main Conjecture for the (global) trivial character. See
theorem

2.2.3
.

To see why the top two rows are exact on the left, first notice that
\text{{H}}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{?}[\mathfrak{p}])=0
for all
?=\tilde{\omega},f
and
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}
if
\tilde{\mathbf{1}}
is globally non-trivial (it’s
\mathbf{F}
otherwise). This gives exactness of the first row. Now
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}[\mathfrak{p}])=\mathbf{F}
and
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])=\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f})[\mathfrak{p}]=\mathbf{F}
because we are in Case I.

Thus the map

\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])

is injective. Adding the local conditions
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}]})=0,\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})=\mathbf{F}
, we get an exact sequence

0\to\mathbf{F}\to\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}]})}\xrightarrow{g_{\overline{v}}}\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])}{\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]})}.\qed

For later use, we remark that we also get from the above argument that

{\operatorname{coker}}(g_{\overline{v}})\cong{\operatorname{coker}}(\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])).

1.4.1

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{f}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{f[\mathfrak{p}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{f}\text{[$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{p}$]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{f}}

which agrees with Case I.

\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f})=0
also implies
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}])=0
, so we get an exact sequence

0\to\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\mathbf{1}}}[\mathfrak{p}])=\mathbf{F}\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{\tilde{\omega}}[\mathfrak{p}])\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f}[\mathfrak{p}]).

This is exactly the final ingredient in the proof of
lemma

1.4.4
. Now exactly the same arguments as in Case I give the desired relation.
∎

1.5.
Comparison I: Algebraic Iwasawa invariants

We’re now ready to state the main result of the algebraic side.

Let

\mathcal{P}_{w}(f)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}P_{w}(\ell^{-1}\gamma_{w})\in\Lambda

with
V_{f}=T_{f}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
and
P_{w}=\det(1-{\operatorname{Frob}}_{w}X\mid V_{f,I_{w}})
.

Recall that we have fixed the ordering of the characters appearing in the following decomposition:

0\to\mathbf{F}(\varphi(=\tilde{\omega}))\to\overline{\rho}_{f}\to\mathbf{F}(\psi(=\tilde{\mathbf{1}}))\to 0.

Theorem 1.5.1
.

Assume that
\varphi|_{G_{p}}=\omega
and
\varphi|_{G_{K}}\neq\omega
.

Then the module
\mathfrak{X}_{f}
is
\Lambda
-torsion with
\mu(\mathfrak{X}_{f})=0
and

\lambda(\mathfrak{X}_{f})=\lambda(\mathfrak{X}_{\varphi})+\lambda(\mathfrak{X}_{\psi})+\sum_{w\in S}\big\{\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{w}(\varphi))+\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{w}(\psi))-\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{w}(f))\big\}.

In the case where
\varphi|_{G_{K}}=\omega
, the same results hold except that the relation between
\Lambda
-invariants now becomes

\lambda(\mathfrak{X}_{f})+1=\lambda(\mathfrak{X}_{\varphi})+\lambda(\mathfrak{X}_{\psi})+\sum_{w\in S}\big\{\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{w}(\varphi))+\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{w}(\psi))-\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{w}(f))\big\}.

Proof.

With our
theorem

1.4.1
and
remark

1.4.2
, the proof is the same as that of
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 1.5.1]
.
∎

2.
Analytic side

Let
f\in S_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
with
k=2r
be a newform,
p\nmid 2N
and
K
an imaginary quadratic field satisfying the Heegner hypothesis. As on the algebraic side, we assume
p=v\overline{v}
is split in
K
. We further assume that the discriminant
D_{K}
of
K
is odd and
D_{K}\neq-3
. Recall that
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
denotes the anticyclotomic Iwasawa algebra, and set
\Lambda^{\operatorname{nr}}=\Lambda\hat{\otimes}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{{\operatorname{nr}}}
, for
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{{\operatorname{nr}}}
the completion of the ring of integers of the maximal unramified extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
. This is where the BDP
p
-adic
L
-function lives.

In this section, we continue to study the case
\overline{\rho}_{f}^{\mathrm{ss}}=\mathbf{F}(\varphi)\oplus\mathbf{F}(\psi)
. Note that the cases when
\varphi|_{G_{p}}=\mathbf{1}
(resp.
\omega
) but
\varphi|_{G_{K}}\neq\mathbf{1}
(resp.
\omega
) are already studied in
[
CGLS22
]
. We therefore only state the theorems we need and explain how to extend the results to the case where
\varphi|_{G_{K}}=\mathbf{1}
.

2.1.
p
-adic
L
-functions

2.1.1.
The Bertolini–Darmon–Prasanna
p
-adic
L
-functions.

Thanks to the Heegner hypothesis, we can fix an integral ideal
\mathfrak{N}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
with

\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{N}\simeq\mathbf{Z}/N.

Proposition 2.1.1

(
p
-adic interpolation property)
.

There exists an element
\mathcal{L}_{f}\in\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}
characterized by the following interpolation property: If
\hat{\xi}\in\mathfrak{X}_{p^{\infty}}
is the
p
-adic avatar of a Hecke character
\xi
of infinity type
(n,-n)
with
n\geq 0
and
p
-power conductor, then

\mathscr{L}_{f}(\hat{\xi})=\frac{\Omega_{p}^{4n}}{\Omega_{K}^{4n}}\cdot\frac{4\Gamma(n+\frac{k}{2})\Gamma(n-\frac{k}{2}+1)\xi^{-1}(\mathfrak{N}^{-1})}{(2\pi)^{2n+1}(\sqrt{D_{K}})^{2n-1}}\cdot(1-a_{p}(f)p^{-r}\xi_{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}}(p)+\xi_{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}}(p^{2})p^{-1})^{2}\cdot L\big(f/K,\xi,\tfrac{k}{2}\big).

Here and in the following
p
-adic units may be ignored in the expressions.

Proof.

This is proved in
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 2.1.1]
, using results from
[
CH18
]
. We remark here that the assumption
p\nmid(2k-1)!
can be directly removed from
op. cit.
so for arbitrary weights we would be able to study all odd primes.

More precisely,
[
CH18
, Prop. 3.8]
gives

\mathscr{L}_{f}(\hat{\xi})=\Omega_{p}^{2k+2n}\cdot L^{\operatorname{alg}}(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi_{K}\otimes\psi\xi)\cdot e_{\mathfrak{p}}(f,\psi\xi)\cdot\xi(\mathfrak{N}^{-1})\cdot 2^{\#A(\psi)+3}c_{0}\varepsilon(f)\cdot u_{K}^{2}\sqrt{D_{K}},

where

L^{\operatorname{alg}}(\tfrac{1}{2},\pi_{K}\otimes\xi)=\frac{\Gamma(n+r)\Gamma(n-r+1)}{2(2\pi)^{2n+1}(\sqrt{D_{K}})^{2n}}\frac{L(\frac{1}{2},\pi_{K}\otimes\xi)}{\Omega_{K}^{4n}},

since
\operatorname{Im}(\vartheta)=\frac{\sqrt{D_{K}}}{2}
.
Note that we can take
c=c_{0}=1
for our application to the weight
2
BSD formula (see for example
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 5.1.3]
where the summation is over
\sigma\in{\operatorname{Gal}}(H/K)
), so in particular
p\nmid c_{0}
and

e_{\mathfrak{p}}(f,\xi)=(1-a_{p}(f)p^{-r}\xi_{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}}(p)+\xi_{\overline{\mathfrak{p}}}(p^{2})p^{-1})^{2}.

Also notice that
u_{K}=1
if
D_{K}<-4
,
A(\psi)=1
since
(D_{K},N)=1
. The global root number
\varepsilon(f)=\pm 1
is ignored since it’s a unit. Now as in
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 2.1.1]
, put
\eta^{-1}\xi
in place of
\xi
for a Hecke character
\eta
of infinity type
(\frac{k}{2},-\frac{k}{2})
, and notice that
\eta(\mathfrak{N})
is also a unit since
p\nmid N
, we get the desired interpolation property.
∎

Remark 2.1.2
.

We mention that the period
\Omega_{K}
is denoted by
\Omega_{\infty}
in
[
CGLS22
]
. But as is pointed out in
[
CGS23
, Remark 2.3.2]
, the
\Omega_{\infty}
defined in the next theorem (coming from Katz, de Shalit and Hida–Tilouine) is different from the
\Omega_{K}
in this proposition (coming from
[
BDP13
]
) and they satisfy the relation
\Omega_{\infty}=2\pi i\cdot\Omega_{K}
.

2.1.2.
Katz
p
-adic
L
-functions.

Theorem 2.1.3
.

There exists an element
\mathcal{L}_{f}\in\Lambda^{\operatorname{nr}}
characterized by the following interpolation property: For every character
\xi
of
\Gamma
crystalline at both
v
and
\bar{v}
and corresponding to a Hecke character of
K
of infinity type
(n,-n)
with
n\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}
and
n\equiv 0
(mod
p-1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\vartheta}(\xi)=\frac{\Omega_{p}^{2n}}{\Omega_{\infty}^{2n}}\cdot 4\Gamma(n+\tfrac{k}{2})\cdot\frac{(2\pi i)^{n-\frac{k}{2}}}{\sqrt{D_{K}}^{n-\frac{k}{2}}}\cdot(1-\vartheta^{-1}(p)\xi^{-1}(v))\cdot(1-\vartheta(p)\xi(\bar{v})p^{-1})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\ell\mid C}(1-\vartheta(\ell)\xi(w)\ell^{-1})\cdot L(\vartheta_{K}\xi\mathbf{N}_{K}^{\frac{k}{2}},0).

Proof.

See
[
Kri16
, Theorem 27]
. Similarly as in
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 2.1.2]
, we take
\chi=\vartheta_{K}\xi\mathbf{N}_{K}^{\frac{k}{2}},k_{1}=n+\frac{k}{2},k_{2}=-n+\frac{k}{2}
(note that the infinity type of the Hecke characters in these
2
papers differ by a sign. Also the weight in
[
Kri16
]
is
k
while the weight in
[
CGLS22
]
is
2k
).
∎

2.2.
Comparison II: Analytic Iwasawa invariants

Theorem 2.2.1
.

\mathcal{L}_{f}\equiv(\mathscr{E}^{\iota}_{\varphi,\psi})^{2}\cdot(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi})^{2}\pmod{p\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}}.

Proof.

See
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 2.2.1]
. Note that when
\varphi|_{G_{K}}\neq\mathbf{1}
, we always have full Eisenstein descent by
[
Kri16
]
. If
\varphi|_{G_{K}}=\mathbf{1}
, we would only get partial Eisenstein descent in some cases. However, the proof in
[
CGLS22
]
essentially only requires partial Eisenstein descent because only the constant term of
G
(a certain Eisenstein series in
loc. cit.
) can be different and the
p
-depletion of
G
does not see the constant term.

We remark here that the formula in
[
Kri16
, Theorem 37]
now becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{G}(\xi)=\frac{\Omega_{p}^{2n}}{\Omega_{K}^{2n}}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(n+\frac{k}{2})\varphi^{-1}(\sqrt{-D_{K}})\xi(\bar{\mathfrak{t}})t}{\mathfrak{g}(\varphi)}\cdot\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{n+\frac{k}{2}}\sqrt{D_{K}}^{n-\frac{k}{2}}}

\displaystyle\times\Xi_{\xi^{-1}\mathbf{N}_{K}^{-\frac{k}{2}}}(\varphi,\psi\omega^{-1},N_{+},N_{-},N_{0})\cdot L(\varphi_{K}\xi\mathbf{N}_{K}^{\frac{k}{2}},0),

where we take
\chi^{-1}=\xi\mathbf{N}_{K}^{\frac{k}{2}},\ \psi_{1}=\varphi,\ \psi_{2}=\varphi^{-1}=\psi\omega^{-1}
, and
j=n-\frac{k}{2}
.
Here we’re using
\mathbf{N}_{K}(\bar{\mathbf{t}})=1
is a unit since
p\nmid c
because
t\mid N
.

From
remark

2.1.2
, the above formula becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{G}(\xi)=\frac{\Omega_{p}^{2n}}{\Omega_{\infty}^{2n}}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(n+\frac{k}{2})\varphi^{-1}(\sqrt{-D_{K}})\xi(\bar{\mathfrak{t}})t}{\mathfrak{g}(\varphi)}\cdot\frac{(2\pi i)^{n-\frac{k}{2}}}{\sqrt{D_{K}}^{n-\frac{k}{2}}}

\displaystyle\times\Xi_{\xi^{-1}\mathbf{N}_{K}^{-\frac{k}{2}}}(\varphi,\psi\omega^{-1},N_{+},N_{-},N_{0})\cdot L(\varphi_{K}\xi\mathbf{N}_{K}^{\frac{k}{2}},0),

which agrees with the formula in
[
CGLS22
, eq. (2.7)]
.
∎

Combining the above inputs, as in
[
CGLS22
]
, we obtain the following:

Theorem 2.2.2
.

Assume that
\overline{\rho}_{f}^{\mathrm{ss}}=\mathbf{F}(\varphi)\oplus\mathbf{F}(\psi)
as
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
-modules, with the characters
\varphi
,
\psi
labeled so that
p\nmid cond(\varphi)
. Then
\mu(\mathcal{L}_{f})=0
and

\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{f})=\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi})+\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{\psi})+\sum_{w\in S}\{\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{w}(\varphi))+\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{w}(\psi))-\lambda(\mathcal{P}_{w}(f))\}.

Theorem 2.2.3
.

Assume that
\overline{\rho}_{f}^{ss}=\mathbf{F}(\psi)\oplus\mathbf{F}(\varphi)
. Then
\mu(\mathcal{L}_{f})=\mu(\mathfrak{X}_{f})=0
and

\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{f})=\lambda(\mathfrak{X}_{f}).

Proof.

The proof relies on the one-variable Iwasawa Main Conjectures for the characters which is already explained in
theorem

1.2.2
. We will add a little more detail. When
\varphi|_{G_{K}}\notin\{\mathbf{1},\omega\}
, the proofs are identical to those in
[
CGLS22
]
. Note also that in those cases the results on the algebraic side (see
theorem

1.5.1
) are also compatible. We explain how to modify the arguments to the case where
\psi|_{G_{K}}=\mathbf{1}
.

It is essentially proved in
[
Rub91
]
that for any character
\vartheta
of
G_{K}
, we have

{\operatorname{Char}}(\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,M_{\vartheta}))={\operatorname{Char}}(U_{\infty}/\bar{\mathscr{C}_{\infty}})^{\vartheta},

where
U_{\infty}
and
\mathscr{C}_{\infty}
are the local units and elliptic units defined in
op. cit.
As is mentioned in the remarks after Theorem 4.1 there, the further identification

{\operatorname{Char}}(U_{\infty}/\bar{\mathscr{C}_{\infty}})^{\vartheta}={\operatorname{Char}}(\mathcal{L}_{\vartheta})

is done in
[
CW78
]
. Notice that they need to assume (i)
p>3
; (ii)
p
is not anomalous for their elliptic curve
E
; and (iii)
i\neq 0
where
i
is the power of
\chi
acting on the isotypic components
(U_{\infty}/\bar{\mathscr{C}_{\infty}})^{(i)}
, with
\chi
giving the canonical action of
{\operatorname{Gal}}(K(E[\pi])/K)
on
E[\pi]
. Notice that our mod
\mathfrak{p}
character
\vartheta
is the reduction of
\chi
, where the trivial character
\mathbf{1}
corresponds to
i=0
and
\omega
corresponds to
i=1
.

The above assumptions are not needed in a more general theorem
[
dS87
, III.1.10]
of de Shalit, but the result is different when
\vartheta|_{G_{K}}=\mathbf{1}
. We remark here that the
i=0
case can also be studied directly using the idea of
[
Yag82
, Lemma 28]
where
(i_{1},i_{2})=(0,0)
, which also yields

{\operatorname{Char}}(U_{\infty}/\bar{\mathscr{C}_{\infty}})^{\mathbf{1}}={\operatorname{Char}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{1}}\cdot T),

showing that
\lambda(\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbf{1}})=\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{1}})+1
. Together with
theorem

1.5.1
, this yields the equation
\lambda(\mathfrak{X}_{f})=\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{f})
, as expected.
∎

Remark 2.2.4
.

The above arguments imply that the standard Iwasawa Main Conjectures for the trivial character is different from others, namely the algebraic
\lambda
-invariant is bigger than the analytic
\lambda
-invariant by
1
. Actually, it is always predicted that some global torsion terms should appear (see, for example,
[
Gre94
]
). However, in our setting, even if
f
has global torsion, it must be finite (from
proposition

1.3.4
(iii)) and will not affect the
\lambda
-invariants, so our use of Perrin-Riou’s formula in section
1.4
is justified.

3.
Proofs of the Iwasawa Main Conjectures

We will directly adopt the notations and the arguments from
[
CGLS22
]
to prove the main theorems of this paper, some of which are further generalized in
[
CGS23
]
. More precisely, we will prove two Iwasawa Main Conjectures at a time. In the Kolyvagin system argument, we need to replace their assumption
E(K)[p]=0
by
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,\overline{\rho}_{f})=0
so that their arguments apply to arbitrary weight modular forms. By the arguments in the beginning of
section

1.4
, we are allowed to make this assumption. We will list the intermediate results we need and comment on what should and should not be changed.

Let
f\in S_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))^{\operatorname{new}}
be a newform and let
\mathbf{Z}[f]
be the coefficient ring of
f
with field of fractions
\mathbf{Q}(f)
. Let
F
be a finite extension of the completion of
\mathbf{Q}(f)
at a chosen prime above
p\nmid 2N
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
. Let
\pi\in\mathcal{O}
be a uniformizer and
\mathbf{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}/\pi
be the residue field of
\mathcal{O}
. Throughout this section, assume that

(h.1)

\text{{H}}^{0}(K,\overline{\rho}_{f})=0.

As before,
\Gamma={\operatorname{Gal}}(K_{\infty}/K)
denote the Galois group of the anticyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension of
K
. We let
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to R^{\times}
be a character with values in the ring of integers
R
of a finite extension
\Phi/\mathbf{Q}_{p}
. Let

r\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\operatorname{rk}}_{\mathcal{O}}R.

Let
\rho_{f}\colon G_{\mathbf{Q}}\to{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(F)
be the unique semisimple
p
-adic Galois representation unramified outside
pN
and such that
\rho_{f}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{\ell})
has characteristic polynomial

T^{2}-a_{\ell}\ell^{1-\frac{k}{2}}T+\ell

for all
\ell\nmid pN
.

Let
T
be an Galois stable lattice and consider the
G_{K}
-modules

T_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}T\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}R(\alpha),\ \ V_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}T\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\Phi,\ \ A_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}T\otimes_{R}\Phi/R\cong V_{\alpha}/T_{\alpha}.

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
be a topological generator, and set
C_{\alpha}\coloneq\begin{cases}v_{p}(\alpha(\gamma)-\alpha^{-1}(\gamma))&\alpha\neq\alpha^{-1},\\
0&\alpha=\alpha^{-1}.\end{cases}

One last thing we need to explain is how to define the filtration on
V_{f},T_{f}
and
A_{f}
. Recall that in the case of elliptic curves one defines
{\operatorname{Fil}}_{w}^{+}(T_{p}E)
as the kernel of the reduction map
T_{p}E\to T_{p}\tilde{E}
at a place
w
of
K
above
p
. For
p
-ordinary modular forms (good ordinary or multiplicative), one can define
{\operatorname{Fil}}_{w}^{+}(T_{f})
on any Galois stable lattice
T_{f}
of
V_{f}
(without self-dual twist) by the characterization that
{\operatorname{Fil}}^{-}(T_{f})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}T_{f}/{\operatorname{Fil}}^{+}(T_{f})
is the unique quotient of
T_{f}
that is unramified. However, note that after a self-dual twist for weight
k>2
, the
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
action on
{\operatorname{Fil}}^{-}(T_{f})
(by abuse of notation) will be given by an unramified character multiplied by a non-trivial power of the cyclotomic character
\chi
.

One then defines the filtration on
T_{\alpha}
,
V_{\alpha}
,
A_{\alpha}
,
\mathbf{T}
and
M_{f}
(or
\mathbf{A}
) in the same way as they do in
[
CGLS22
]
, and use these in the definition of the ordinary Selmer groups.

The first remarkable result from
[
CGLS22
]
is the following theorem generalizing
[
How04
, Theorem 2.2.10]
.

Theorem 3.0.1
.

Suppose
\alpha\neq 1
and that there is a Kolyvagin system
\kappa_{\alpha}=\{\kappa_{\alpha,n}\}_{n\in\mathcal{N}}\in\mathbf{KS}(T_{\alpha},\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{ord}}},\mathcal{L}_{f})
with
\kappa_{1}
being non-torsion. Then
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{ord}}}}(K,T_{\alpha})
has rank one, and there is a finite
R
-module
M_{\alpha}
such that

\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{ord}}}}(K,A_{\alpha})\cong(\Phi/R)\oplus M_{\alpha}\oplus M_{\alpha}

with

{\operatorname{length}}_{R}(M_{\alpha})\leq{\operatorname{length}}_{R}(\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{ord}}}}(K,T_{\alpha})/R\cdot\kappa_{\alpha,1})+E_{\alpha}

for some constant
E_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
depending only on
C_{\alpha},T_{f}
, and
{\operatorname{rk}}_{\mathcal{O}}(R)
.

In
[
CGS23
, section 6]
, the above result was generalized so that the error term can be made independent of
\alpha
, at the expense of requiring
\alpha
to be ‘sufficiently close to
1
’. More precisely, they have

Theorem 3.0.2
.

Assume
\alpha\equiv 1\pmod{\varpi^{m}}
. Then there exist non-negative integers
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{E}
depending only on
T_{p}E
and
{\operatorname{rk}}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(R)
such that if
m\geq\mathcal{M}
and if there is a Kolyvagin system
\kappa_{\alpha}=\{\kappa_{\alpha,n}\}_{n\in\mathcal{N}}\in\mathbf{KS}(T_{\alpha},\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{ord}}},\mathcal{L}_{f})
with
\kappa_{1}
being non-torsion. Then
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{ord}}}}(K,T_{\alpha})
has rank one, and there is a finite
R
-module
M_{\alpha}
such that

\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{ord}}}}(K,A_{\alpha})\cong(\Phi/R)\oplus M_{\alpha}\oplus M_{\alpha}

with

{\operatorname{length}}_{R}(M_{\alpha})\leq{\operatorname{length}}_{R}(\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{ord}}}}(K,T_{\alpha})/R\cdot\kappa_{\alpha,1})+\mathcal{E}

and the ‘error term’
\mathcal{E}
is independent of
m
.

We mention that
theorem

3.0.1
(in combination with our
theorem

1.5.1
and
theorem

2.2.3
) is sufficient as an ingredient for us to get a desired BSD formula in rank
1
, but together with
theorem

3.0.2
it leads to a stronger result on the anticyclotomic Main Conjecture, in the sense that the divisibility in
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 3.4.1]
holds more generally without the need to invert
\gamma-1
(see
[
CGS23
, Theorem 6.5.1]
).

Thanks to the highly axiomatized arguments from
[
CGLS22
]
,
[
CGS23
]
, we only need to explain how to get the error term
C_{1}
in
op. cit.
, as everything else can be directly extended to our more general setting.

According to the proof of
[
CGLS22
, Proposition 3.6]
, we only need to find one non-trivial scalar matrix in
\mathcal{O}^{\times}\cap{\operatorname{im}}(\rho_{f}|_{G_{K_{\infty}}})
.

Lemma 3.0.3
.

The intersection
U=\mathcal{O}^{\times}\cap{\operatorname{im}}(\rho_{f}|_{G_{K_{\infty}}})
contains infinitely many scalar matrices. Here by abuse of notation
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
denotes
\mathcal{O}^{\times}\cdot I_{2}
, the invertible scalar matrices in
{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathcal{O})
.

Proof.

Note that by the discussion above
[
CGLS22
, Lemma 3.3.3]
, we have

\rho_{f}(G_{\mathbf{Q}})=\rho_{f}(G_{K_{\infty}}).

Since
\rho_{f}
is a Hodge–Tate representation, Bogomolov in
[
Bog80
]
showed the image (possibly after embedding
{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(F)
in
{\operatorname{GL}}_{2n}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
for some
n
) is open in its algebraic envelope. Since
\rho_{f}
is irreducible by
[
Rib77
]
, so is its natural projection in
{\operatorname{PGL}}_{2}(F)
. We also know
{\operatorname{im}}(\rho_{f})
must be infinite. Actually, there will be infinitely many determinants since
\det\rho_{f}=\chi^{k-1}
from
op. cit.
Now by the classification of all algebraic subgroups of
{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(F)
(see
[
NvdPT08
]
, whose results work over any algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
), we see that the algebraic envelope of
{\operatorname{im}}(\rho_{f})
must be
F^{\times}
times a minimal group (in Theorem
4
of
op. cit.
, only (2)(a)(b) have infinitely many determinants, yet they are reducible), therefore it must contain infinitely many scalar matrices. So does the image.
∎

Remark 3.0.4
.

It is mentioned in
[
CGLS22
]
that
f
has no CM by
K
under their assumptions. When
f
has no CM, there is a more straightforward proof of this openness result, using Momose’s results (see for example
[
Rib85
]
) generalizing the open image results of Serre.

For
U=\mathcal{O}^{\times}\cap{\operatorname{im}}(\rho_{f})
as in
lemma

3.0.3
, let

C_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\min\{v_{p}(u-1):u\in U\}.

Since
U
is an open subgroup of
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
,
0\leq C_{1}<\infty
.

The rest of the proof of
theorem

3.0.1
and
theorem

3.0.2
can be directly borrowed from the corresponding papers. We next explain how to apply Iwasawa theory to prove the Main Conjectures. First we study a consequence of
theorem

3.0.1
,
theorem

3.0.2
.

Theorem 3.0.5
.

Suppose there is a Kolyvagin system
\kappa\in\mathbf{KS}(\mathbf{T},\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda},\mathscr{L}_{f})
where
\kappa_{1}
is non-torsion. Then
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})
has
\Lambda
-rank one, and there is a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda
-module
M
such that

(i)

\mathcal{X}\sim\Lambda\oplus M\oplus M
,

(ii)

{\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(M)
divides
{\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\mathbf{T})/\Lambda\kappa_{1})
in
\Lambda
.

Proof.

With
theorem

3.0.1
and
theorem

3.0.2
, this theorem follows exactly as in
[
CGLS22
]
and
[
CGS23
]
, using specializations at height one primes of
\Lambda
. We mention that in the aforementioned papers the divisibilities hold only after inverting
p
, which is sufficient to their applications, but the computation works for the prime
p
too, as is readily explained in
[
How04
, Theorem 2.2.10]
.
∎

The next result allows us to construct a non-trivial Kolyvagin system.

Theorem 3.0.6
.

Assume
f
has weight
2r
where
r
is odd. There exists a Kolyvagin system
\kappa^{\operatorname{Hg}}\in\mathbf{KS}(\mathbf{T},\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda},\mathscr{L}_{f})
such that
\kappa^{\operatorname{Hg}}_{1}\in\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})
is non-torsion.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}=\tilde{T}\otimes\Lambda
where
\tilde{T}
is the canonical Galois stable lattice in the self-dual Galois representation
V_{f}
constructed in
[
Nek92
, Section 3]
(see also the beginning of
[
CH18
, Section 4.2]
). Note that this
\tilde{T}
is in general different from our chosen
T
so (
h.1
) may not hold for
\tilde{T}
, though they do have the same residual representation. In what follows, we use a tilde to denote relevant modules associated to the canonical lattice. We will follow the construction in
[
CH18
]
and
[
LV16
]
to show that one can find a non-trivial Kolyvagin system for
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
, and then use it to get a non-trivial Kolyvagin system on our chosen
\mathbf{T}
.

The construction of Generalized Heegner cycles for
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
and how they form a Kolyvagin system are essentially given in
[
LV16
, Section 4]
. However, we need to further take into consideration some local uncertainty coming from potential local torsion. We explain how to modify their construction to our situation, following
[
CGLS22
]
. As in
op. cit.
, we do not assume
p\nmid h_{K}
or the ‘big image’ result. The first change appears in
[
LV16
, Theorem 2.4]
, where the vanishing of
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty},\tilde{W})
now follows from our
proposition

1.3.4
(iii) (whose proof did not use
\mathbf{F}(\omega)
is a subrepresentation of
\rho_{f}
so it works for
\tilde{T}
too). Note that for the construction of Kolyvagin systems, the axioms (H.0)–(H.5) are not needed. Their Lemma 4.2 still works if we first replace
p
with a generator
\pi
of
\mathfrak{p}
, but this then implies the original result for
p
. Finally, the map induced by restriction

\text{{H}}^{1}(K[n],\tilde{\mathbf{T}}/I_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{T}})^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\leftarrow\text{{H}}^{1}(K,\tilde{\mathbf{T}}/I_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{T}})

may not be an isomorphism since we do not assume (
h.1
). However, we claim that it has kernel and cokernel bounded independent of
n
.

Note that we have the identifications
\text{{H}}^{1}(K,\tilde{\mathbf{T}}/I_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{T}})\simeq\varinjlim_{i}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{i},V_{f}/\tilde{T}[I_{n}])
and
\text{{H}}^{1}(K[n],\tilde{\mathbf{T}}/I_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{T}})\simeq\varinjlim_{i}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{i}[n],V_{f}/\tilde{T}[I_{n}])
by Shapiro’s lemma.

As in the proof of
[
DLF21
, Proposition 7.17]
, there is a canonical isomorphism
{\operatorname{Gal}}(K_{i}(V_{f}/\tilde{T}[I_{n}])/K_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\operatorname{Gal}}(\mathbf{Q}(V_{f}/\tilde{T}[I_{n}])/\mathbf{Q})
(using that
K_{i}
and
\mathbf{Q}(V_{f}/\tilde{T}[I_{n}])
are linearly disjoint because of their ramification). Hence the fixed submodule
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{i}[n],V_{f}/\tilde{T}[I_{n}])
under these groups are contained in
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty},V_{f}/\tilde{T}[I_{n}])\subseteq\mathcal{O}/\pi^{N}
, which is uniformly bounded independent of
n
and
i
by
proposition

1.3.4
.

Hence in the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=\text{{H}}^{p}(K_{i}[n]/K_{i},\text{{H}}^{q}(K_{i}[n],V_{f}/\tilde{T}[I_{n}]))\Rightarrow E^{p+q}=\text{{H}}^{p+q}(K_{i},V_{f}/\tilde{T}[I_{n}]),

the
E_{2}^{p,0}
are of uniformly bounded exponent. Letting
p=1,2
, the
5
-term exact sequence gives us the desired result for the kernel and cokernel of the restriction.

Thus there is a power
p^{N}
for some
N\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
independent of
n
(one can take
N=0
if (
h.1
) holds for
\tilde{T}
, for similar reason to that in
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 4.1.1]
combined with
proposition

1.3.3
) such that
p^{N}\tilde{\kappa}_{n}\in\text{{H}}^{1}(K[n],\tilde{\mathbf{T}}/I_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{T}})^{\mathcal{G}(n)}
has a unique image
\kappa_{n}
in
\text{{H}}^{1}(K,\tilde{\mathbf{T}}/I_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{T}})
. Here the
\tilde{\kappa}_{n}
still denotes the class in
[
LV16
, eq. (4.2)]
. The construction in
[
LV16
, Section 4]
now almost gives a Kolyvagin system in our setting, and here we explain some modifications of their arguments. Note that if their arguments work for their
\kappa_{n}
, they also work for our
\kappa_{n}
(except for the differences we explain below) since multiplying by a
p
-power does not affect the local behaviors.

First, we give a different reasoning for the right vertical map
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{n}^{-})\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K[n]_{w},\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{n}^{-})
in the diagram (22) in
loc. cit.
to be injective. In fact, we will directly adopt the ideas of Howard in
[
How04
, Lemma 2.3.4, Case (iii)]
. One can identify
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{n}^{-})=\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},\varprojlim_{m}{\operatorname{Ind}}^{K_{m}}_{K}(\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n}))
with
\varprojlim_{m}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},{\operatorname{Ind}}^{K_{m}}_{K}(\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n}))
which by Shapiro’s lemma is further identified with
\varprojlim_{m}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{m,v},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})
, where
K_{m}
is the
m
-th layer in the anticyclotomic tower
K_{\infty}/K
and by abuse of notation we denote a place in
K_{m}
above
v
still by
v
. Similarly,
\text{{H}}^{1}(K[n]_{w},\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{n}^{-})
can be identified with
\varprojlim_{m}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{m}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})
for
w
a prime of
K_{m}[n]
above
v
. Now from the inflation-restriction exact sequence the kernel of the above map is identified with

\varprojlim_{m}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{m}[n]_{w}/K_{m,v},\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{m}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})).

We first claim that for all large
m
,
{\operatorname{Gal}}(K_{m}[n]_{w}/K_{m,v})\cong{\operatorname{Gal}}(K_{\infty}[m]_{w}/K_{\infty,v})
. This follows from the fact that for all large
m
,
v
is totally ramified in
K_{\infty}
and hence
K_{\infty}
and
K_{m}[n]
are linearly disjoint (at least when
p\nmid h_{K}
, otherwise we can increase
m
to kill the finite difference). Now for any
1\neq n\in\mathcal{N}
(the case
n=1
will be treated separately), we have
p\mid I_{n}
by the choice of Kolyvagin primes (see e.g. the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{E}
before
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 3.2.1]
), so
\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n}
is finite and
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{m}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})
must also stabilize for large
m
. In other words, for sufficiently large
m
, we have

\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{m}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})=\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{m+1}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})=\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})

Consequently, for
n\neq 1
the above kernel can be identified with

\varprojlim_{m}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\infty}[n]_{w}/K_{\infty,v},\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})).

Since the inverse limit is with respect to the corestriction on the above
H^{0}
groups (hence is identified with the norm map), and
G_{K_{m}[n]_{w}}
acts on
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{m+1}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})
trivially by the above identification, we can interpret the inverse limit as one with respect to multiplication by
p
on the fixed module

\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\infty}[n]_{w}/K_{\infty,v},\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})).

Since the module is finite as a consequence of the finiteness of both
{\operatorname{Gal}}(K_{\infty}[n]_{w}/K_{\infty,v})
and
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty}[n]_{w},\tilde{T}^{-}/I_{n})
, the inverse limit is trivial.

Second, the cokernel of the trace map
\text{{H}}^{0}(K[n]_{w},\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{-})\to\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{v},\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{-})
in
[
LV16
, Lemma 4.12]
can fail to be trivial (their ‘
\mathbf{A}
’ is our ‘
M_{f}
’). In other words, their
\kappa_{n}
may not lie in the desired Selmer group
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}(n)}(K,\tilde{\mathbf{T}}/I_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{T}})
. For example, when
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{v},\tilde{A}^{-})\neq 0
(e.g. when
\mathbf{F}(\tilde{\mathbf{1}})
is a non-split subrepresentation of
\overline{\rho}_{f}
or when the sequence
0\to\mathbf{F}(\tilde{\omega})\to\overline{\rho}_{f}\to\mathbf{F}(\tilde{\mathbf{1}})\to 0
is locally split, or more generally in the ‘anomalous case’), by local Euler characteristic formula one can show that
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},\tilde{A}^{-})[\mathfrak{p}]
and hence
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},\tilde{A}^{-})\neq 0
. This non-vanishing phenomenon can occur even in the residually irreducible case (but it can be avoided if one assumes
a_{p}\neq 1\pmod{\mathfrak{p}}
).

The way to fix it is to use a fixed
p
-power independent of
n
to kill the cokernel so that, after multiplying the classes
\kappa_{n}
by this
p
-power, they belong to the Selmer groups and form a Kolyvagin system. We must show the existence of such
p
-powers. To bound the cokernel of the trace map, it suffices to bound the codomain
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{v},\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{-})
.

We argue that
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{v},\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{-})
is finite similarly as what we do in
proposition

1.3.4
. When the weight of
f
is
2r>2
, our self-dual twist has the effect that the action of
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
on
V^{-}
is an unramified character multiplied by a non-trivial power of the cyclotomic character. Since
K_{\infty}
is the anticyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
extension,
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty,v},V^{-})=0
and hence
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{v},\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{-})\simeq\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty,v},\tilde{A}^{-})
is finite. When
f
has weight
2
, the finiteness follows from passing to abelian varieties.

Thus we have shown that
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{v},\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{-})
is finite (necessarily independent from
n
) and so is the cokernel of the trace map. Hence there is an integer
t\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
p^{t}
annihilates the cokernel. It is then not hard to see that
p^{t}\kappa_{n}\in\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}(n)}(K,\tilde{\mathbf{T}}/I_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{T}})
in the notation of
[
LV16
]
for all
1\neq n\in\mathcal{N}
. For
n=1
, the same result follows from the fact that the image of
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
in
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},\tilde{\mathbf{T}})
lies in
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\operatorname{ord}}(K_{v},\tilde{\mathbf{T}})
(see Lemma 4.12 in
loc. cit.
). Moreover, the Kolyvagin system relation is also preserved by multiplying every class with a fixed
p
-power. We define
\kappa^{\widetilde{{\operatorname{Hg}}}}_{n}
to be the modification of
p^{t}\kappa_{n}
(one can take
t=0
if the trace map is indeed surjective. For example, this is the case if one assumes
\varphi,\psi|_{G_{p}}\neq\mathbf{1},\omega
) in
[
LV16
, Theorem 4.13]
. Here we assume
r
is odd so the factor
(-1)^{r-1}
in
[
CH18
, (K2)]
disappears and the factors on both sides differ at most by a sign. Then
\kappa^{\widetilde{{\operatorname{Hg}}}}
is a Kolyvagin system for
\tilde{T}
.

That the class is non-trivial follows from
[
CH18
, Proposition 3.9, Theorem 4.9]
. Indeed, the left hand side of the equation in Theorem 4.9 in
op. cit.
is nonzero by Proposition 3.9 there, so the right hand side is nonzero. Therefore the class
z_{f}\neq 0
. But this is just
{\operatorname{cores}}_{K[1]/K}\beta_{0}[1]
in
[
LV16
, Proposition 4.9]
. Therefore from its construction it’s easy to see the first Kolyvagin class
\tilde{\kappa}_{1}
is non-trivial. Since
\kappa_{1}
is the image of
p^{N}\tilde{\kappa}_{1}
in
\text{{H}}^{1}(K,\tilde{\mathbf{T}})
,
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa^{\widetilde{{\operatorname{Hg}}}}_{1}=p^{t}\kappa_{1}
are also non-trivial.

We now explain how to construct a Kolyvagin system on our chosen
T
which satisfies (
h.1
). Let
T^{\prime}=T\cap\tilde{T}
which has finite index in both
T
and
\tilde{T}
. Choose an
m\in\mathbf{Z}
with
p^{m}\tilde{T}\subset T^{\prime}
. Now the map
\tilde{T}\to p^{m}\tilde{T}\to T^{\prime}
induces a map
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\to\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
. Now the classes
\kappa^{\widetilde{{\operatorname{Hg}}}}_{n}\in\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,\tilde{\mathbf{T}}/I_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{T}})
are mapped to their images
\kappa^{{\operatorname{Hg}}^{\prime}}_{n}\in\text{{H}}^{1}(K,\mathbf{T}^{\prime}/I_{n}\mathbf{T}^{\prime})
and it is not hard to check that in fact
\kappa^{{\operatorname{Hg}}^{\prime}}_{n}\in\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,\mathbf{T}^{\prime}/I_{n}\mathbf{T}^{\prime})
by functoriality. Moreover, the Kolyvagin system relation is also preserved, i.e.,
\kappa^{{\operatorname{Hg}}^{\prime}}_{n}
is a Kolyvagin system for
T^{\prime}
. Now the map
T^{\prime}\to T
maps
\kappa^{{\operatorname{Hg}}^{\prime}}_{n}
to
\kappa^{{\operatorname{Hg}}}_{n}\in\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,\mathbf{T}/I_{n}\mathbf{T})
which is a Kolyvagin system for
T
.

Finally, that
\kappa^{\operatorname{Hg}}_{1}
is non-torsion follows from the fact that
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})
is torsion free (see for example
[
How04
, Theorem 2.2.9]
where the condition
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\infty},\overline{\rho}_{f})=0
follows from (
h.1
) and
proposition

1.3.3
).∎

Remark 3.0.7
.

The above theorem is the only place where the assumption that
r
is odd is used. It is not necessary if the
B_{1}
in
[
CH18
, eq. (7.2)]
is zero, e.g., when
\rho_{f}^{*}
is surjective.

We are ready to prove the two Iwasawa Main Conjectures similar to those in
[
CGLS22
]
. Here in the statement one needs to work with a seemingly different Kolyvagin class ‘
\kappa_{\infty}
’ coming from the equivalence between the Main conjectures, but as is noted in
[
CGLS22
, Remark 4.1.3]
,
\kappa_{\infty}
and
\kappa_{1}
generate the same
\Lambda
-submodule in
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})
.

Theorem 3.0.8
.

Assume
f
has weight
2r
where
r
is odd. Assume that
p=v\overline{v}
splits in
K
and
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,\overline{\rho}_{f})=0
Then the following statements hold:

(IMC1)

Both
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})
and
\mathcal{X}=\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,M_{f})^{\vee}
have
\Lambda
-rank one, and the equality

{\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{X}_{{\operatorname{tors}}})={\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})/\Lambda\kappa_{\infty})^{2}

holds in
\Lambda_{ac}
.

(IMC2)

Both
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,\mathbf{T})
and
\mathfrak{X}_{f}=\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,M_{f})^{\vee}
are
\Lambda
-torsion, and the equality

{\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{X}_{f})\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}=(\mathcal{L}_{f})

holds in
\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}
.

Proof.

We first treat the
t=N=0
case in
theorem

3.0.6
. We begin by noting that (IMC1) and (IMC2) are equivalent; in fact, an either side divisibility in one of them is equivalent to a corresponding divisibility in the other (see
[
CGLS22
, Prop. 4.2.1]
and
[
BCK21
, Thm. 5.2]
combined with
proposition

1.3.3
. See also
[
Cas17
, Appendix A]
). Here we should mention that the equivalence is done with the Greenberg Selmer groups, but they generate the same characteristic ideals as the unramified Selmer group do.
theorem

3.0.5
shows one divisibility of (IMC1), which translates to a divisibility of (IMC2). Using the equality between the algebraic and analytic Iwasawa invariants (see
theorem

1.5.1
and
theorem

2.2.3
), the divisibility in (IMC2) is turned into an equality. Hence (IMC1) also holds.

Now if
t>0
or
N>0
, the proof in
[
Cas17
, Appendix A]
would yield a new equivalence involving a fixed
p
-power:

(IMC1’)

Both
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})
and
\mathcal{X}=\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,M_{f})^{\vee}
have
\Lambda
-rank one, and the divisibility

{\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{X}_{{\operatorname{tors}}})\supset{\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})/\Lambda\cdot(p^{t+N}\kappa_{\infty}))^{2}

holds in
\Lambda_{ac}
.

(IMC2’)

Both
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,\mathbf{T})
and
\mathfrak{X}_{f}=\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,M_{f})^{\vee}
are
\Lambda
-torsion, and the divisibility

{\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{X}_{f})\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}\supset(p^{t+N}\mathcal{L}_{f})

holds in
\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}
.

(the same result holds for the opposite divisibilities, but we will not need it). In our situation,
theorem

3.0.5
applied to the Kolyvagin system
\kappa^{\operatorname{Hg}}_{n}=p^{t}\kappa_{n}
(again noting the relation between
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{\infty}
) shows the divisibility in (IMC1’). Hence we obtain (IMC2’) from the equivalence. However, since we know both
\mathfrak{X}_{f}
and
\mathcal{L}_{f}
have vanishing
\mu
-invariants and equal
\lambda
-invariants (all computed independently), we obtain from (IMC2’) exactly the same equality in (IMC2). Now from the equivalence between (IMC1) and (IMC2), we obtain (IMC1) as before.
∎

Remark 3.0.9
.

As is explained in the beginning of
section

1.4
, the above theorem still holds without assuming
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,\overline{\rho}_{f})=0
.

As a corollary of our anticyclotomic Main Conjectures, we also obtain the following Mazur’s Main Conjecture for elliptic curves unconditionally in the Eisenstein case using the main results of
[
CGS23
]
. Let
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}\llbracket{\operatorname{Gal}}(\mathbf{Q}^{\infty}/\mathbf{Q})\rrbracket
be the cyclotomic Iwasawa algebra over
\mathbf{Q}
. Let
\mathfrak{X}_{\operatorname{ord}}(E/\mathbf{Q}_{\infty})
be the Pontryagin dual of the
p
-primary Selmer group
{\operatorname{Sel}}_{p^{\infty}}(E/\mathbf{Q}_{\infty})
for
E
and let
\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\operatorname{MSD}}(E/\mathbf{Q})
be the Mazur–Swinnerton-Dyer
p
-adic
L
-function.

Theorem 3.0.10
.

Let
E/\mathbf{Q}
be an elliptic curve, and let
p>2
be a prime of good reduction for
E
. Suppose that
p
is Eisenstein. Then
\mathfrak{X}_{\operatorname{ord}}(E/\mathbf{Q}_{\infty})
is
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Q}}
-cotorsion with

{\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda_{\mathbf{Q}}}(\mathfrak{X}_{\operatorname{ord}}(E/\mathbf{Q}_{\infty}))=\mathcal{L}_{p}^{\operatorname{MSD}}(E/\mathbf{Q}),

and hence Mazur’s Main Conjecture holds.

Proof.

This is the main result of
[
CGS23
]
. Their technical assumption on the local behaviors of the characters in the semisimplification of
E[p]
can be removed if one replaces the appeal to
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 4.2.2, Corollary 4.2.3]
by our
theorem

3.0.8
and
remark

3.0.9
(specialized to weight
2
case).
∎

4.
Proofs of the
p
-converse and the
p
-part of BSD formula

In this section, we discuss several applications of our results in the previous sections, whose proofs are direct generalizations of those in
[
CGLS22
, § 5]
. We will only consider the weight
2
cases.

4.1.
The
p
-converse theorem

Theorem 4.1.1
.

Let
A/\mathbf{Q}
be a simple RM abelian variety associated to a newform
f
and
\mathfrak{P}\mid p>2
a prime ideal of its endomorphism ring
\mathcal{O}={\operatorname{End}}(A)
of good reduction such that
\rho_{A,\mathfrak{P}}
is reducible.

Then

{\operatorname{corank}}_{\mathcal{O}}{\operatorname{Sel}}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\infty}}(A/\mathbf{Q})=r\in\{0,1\}\implies{\operatorname{rk}}_{\mathcal{O}}A(\mathbf{Q})=r_{\operatorname{an}}(f)=r,

and
\Sha(A/\mathbf{Q})[\mathfrak{P}^{\infty}]
is finite.

Proof.

The proof of
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 5.2.1]
works if one replaces the reference [Mon96, Theorem 1.5] there by
[
Nek18
, Theorem C]
and replaces
[
CGLS22
, Corollary 4.2.3]
by our
theorem

3.0.8
(IMC1).
∎

The
p
-converse theorem has the following applications to Goldfeld’s conjecture in quadratic twist families with a
3
-isogeny where
3
is a prime of good or bad multiplicative reduction. We thank Ari Shnidman for communicating and explaining them to us.

Remark 4.1.2
.

(i)

Better proportions of quadratic twists of (algebraic and analytic) rank
1
in
[
BKOS19
, Theorem 2.5]
for a fixed elliptic curve, in particular a lower bound of
\frac{3}{4}\cdot\frac{5}{12}=\frac{5}{16}=31.25\,\%
in the most advantageous cases (for example, when it’s the curve having Cremona label
19a3
). Note that the proportion claimed in
[
CGLS22
, Remark 5.2.4]
is only for a subfamily (corresponding to good reduction and with their assumptions on the isogeny character) of the twists. Indeed, for the twists
E_{d}
of the curve
E=19a3
, the results in
loc. cit.
only apply when
d\equiv 2\pmod{3}
whereas our result also covers the cases when
d\equiv 1\pmod{3}
, covering at least
\frac{3}{8}+\frac{3}{8}=\frac{3}{4}
of the proportion of the
3
-Selmer rank
1
twists. The twists with
d\equiv 0\pmod{3}
correspond to bad additive reduction and are not covered by our
p
-converse theorems. However, potentially good ordinary primes are studied in our subsequent work
[
KY24
]
and combined with our work, we can get the full lower bound
\frac{5}{12}=41.67\%
of rank
1
twists for the above curve
19a3
.

(ii)

Our result seems not to improve the bounds for rank
0
in
[
BKOS19
]
in the situation of (i), but it allows one to quantify how often the BSD rank conjecture holds. Namely, their results give us that at least
\frac{1}{4}
of the twists have
3
-Selmer rank
0
, hence at least
\frac{3}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{4}=\frac{3}{16}
of them have analytic rank
0
. Together with (i),
\frac{3}{16}+\frac{5}{16}=\frac{1}{2}
of the twists satisfy the BSD rank conjecture.

(iii)

Similar improvements apply to quadratic twist families of abelian varieties
A
of
{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}
-type over
\mathbf{Q}
with a
3
-isogeny, as in
[
BFS23
, Theorem 1.4, Corollary 7.2]
and
[
Shn21
, Theorem 1.5]
. For example, in the context of the latter result (where the abelian varieties all have good reduction at
3
), our
theorem

4.1.1
gives an unconditional lower bound of
\frac{5}{16}
for the proportion of twists with rank
1
over the RM ring.

4.2.
The
p
-part of BSD formula

Theorem 4.2.1
.

Let
E/\mathbf{Q}
be an elliptic curve and
p>2
a prime of good reduction. Assume that
E
admits a cyclic
p
-isogeny with kernel
C=\mathbf{F}_{p}(\varphi)
for some character
\varphi\colon G_{\mathbf{Q}}\to\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}
(equivalently,
E[p]
is reducible). Assume that
r_{\operatorname{an}}(E)\in\{0,1\}
.

Then the
p
-part of the BSD formula holds for
E/\mathbf{Q}
, i.e.,

{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}\Big(\frac{L^{*}(E/\mathbf{Q},1)}{\Omega_{E}\cdot{\operatorname{Reg}}_{E/\mathbf{Q}}}\Big)={\operatorname{ord}}_{p}\Big(\frac{{\operatorname{Tam}}(E/\mathbf{Q})\#\Sha(E/\mathbf{Q})[p^{\infty}]}{(\#E(\mathbf{Q})_{\operatorname{tors}})^{2}}\Big).

Here,
L^{*}(E/\mathbf{Q},1)
denotes the leading Taylor coefficient of
L(E/\mathbf{Q},s)
at
s=1
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 5.3.1]
, where the use of Theorem 4.2.2 in
op. cit.
is replaced by our
theorem

3.0.8
(IMC2). We remark that the assumption that the character is either ramified at
p
and even, or unramified at
p
and odd coming from
[
GV00
]
is now removed by
[
CGS23
]
in both the rank
0
and rank
1
formulae, and the assumption that
\varphi|_{G_{p}}\neq\mathbf{1},\omega
is removed by us.

In particular, we take
k=2
,
r=j=0
and
\chi=\mathbf{N}_{K}
in
[
BDP13
, Theorem 5.13]
. Note that
L_{p}(f,\mathbf{N}_{K})
in
loc. cit.
corresponds
\mathcal{L}_{E}(0)
because it corresponds to the value
L(f/K,\mathbf{N}_{K}^{-1},0)=L(f/K,\mathbf{1},1)=L(f/K,1)
(see
[
BDP13
, section 4]
).

Note that to make the arguments in
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 5.3.1]
work, we need an anticyclotomic control theorem which allows torsion submodules. This is done in Appendix
B
. The formula in
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 5.1.1]
then becomes

\#\mathbf{Z}_{p}/f_{E}(0)=\#\Sha(E/K)[p^{\infty}]\cdot\Bigg(\frac{\#(\mathbf{Z}_{p}/(\frac{1-a_{p}+p}{p})\cdot\log_{\omega_{E}}P)}{[E(K)_{/{\operatorname{tors}}}:\mathbf{Z}\cdot P]_{p}\cdot\#(E(K)_{{\operatorname{tors}}})_{p}}\Bigg)^{2}\times\prod_{w}c_{w}(E/K)_{p},

where
x_{p}\coloneq p^{{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(x)}
denotes the
p
-part of a rational number
x
. Alternatively, one could continue to work with an isogenous curve which has no torsion, and use the invariance of BSD formulae to get the same results with torsion.
We remark that in the control theorem from
[
JSW17
]
, their anticyclotomic Selmer group is actually the ‘Greenberg Selmer group’ for
f
(which agrees with the unramified Selmer group in
[
CGLS22
]
). However, since
\ker({\operatorname{res}}_{M_{f}})
is finite by
lemma

1.3.6
, these two Selmer groups generate the same characteristic ideals.

Together with the above formula coming from the control theorem and the fact that
E(K)[p^{\infty}]=E(\mathbf{Q})[p^{\infty}]\oplus E^{K}(\mathbf{Q})[p^{\infty}]
(because
p>2
), equation (5.7) in
[
CGLS22
]
becomes

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}\Big(\frac{L^{\prime}(E,1)}{{\operatorname{Reg}}(E/\mathbf{Q})\cdot\Omega_{E}\cdot\prod_{l}c_{l}(E/\mathbf{Q})}\Big)-{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(\#\Sha(E/\mathbf{Q}))+2{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(E(\mathbf{Q})_{\operatorname{tors}})

\displaystyle=-\Big({\operatorname{ord}}_{p}\Big(\frac{L(E^{K},1)}{\Omega_{E^{K}}\cdot\prod_{l}c_{l}(E^{K}/\mathbf{Q})}\Big)-{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(\#\Sha(E^{K}/\mathbf{Q}))+2{\operatorname{ord}}_{p}(E^{K}(\mathbf{Q})_{\operatorname{tors}})\Big)

where the right-hand side vanishes by
[
CGLS22
, Theorem 5.1.4]
, a result of Greenberg–Vatsal.
∎

5.
Proof of the Iwasawa Main Conjecture with multiplicative reduction

Let
f\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
be a newform of weight
2
with coefficient field
\mathbf{Q}(f)
. Let
F
be a finite extension of the completion of
\mathbf{Q}(f)
at a chosen prime above
p\neq 2
and let
\mathcal{O}
be its ring of integers. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}
and let
\mathbf{F}=\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}
be the residue field of
\mathcal{O}
. Let
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}\llbracket T\rrbracket
and
\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}=\Lambda\hat{\otimes}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{{\operatorname{nr}}}
. Let
\overline{\rho}_{f}
be the residual representation attached to
f
and assume
\overline{\rho}_{f}
is reducible. Then there are characters
\varphi,\psi
such that there is an exact sequence

(5.1)

0\to\mathbf{F}(\varphi)\to\overline{\rho}_{f}\to\mathbf{F}(\psi)\to 0.

Further assume that
p\mid\mid N
, so
f
has bad multiplicative reduction at
p
. In this section, we prove the Iwasawa Main Conjectures for such forms using Hida families, following the ideas in
[
Ski14
]
.

5.1.
Hida arguments

As in the good reduction case, define the unramified Selmer group for
f
by

\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,M_{f})=\ker\Big\{\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{res}}_{{\operatorname{nr}},f}}\prod_{w\in\Sigma,w\nmid p}\text{{H}}^{1}(I_{w},M_{f})^{G_{w}/I_{w}}\times\text{{H}}^{1}(I_{\overline{v}},M_{f})^{G_{\overline{v}}/I_{\overline{v}}}\Big\}.

For a finite set
\Sigma
of places of
K
which contains
\infty
and the primes above divisors of
N
, and such that the finite places in
\Sigma
are all split in
K
, let
S=\Sigma\setminus\{v,\bar{v},\infty\}
and define the
S
-imprimitive Selmer group for
f
by
