\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{S}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,M_{f})=\ker\Big\{\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{res}}^{S}_{{\operatorname{nr}},f}}\text{{H}}^{1}(I_{\overline{v}},M_{f})^{G_{\overline{v}}/I_{\overline{v}}}\Big\}.

Let
\mathfrak{X}_{f}:=\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,M_{f})^{\vee}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{f}^{S}:=\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{S}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}}(K,M_{f})^{\vee}
denote the dual Selmer groups.

According to
[
Ski14
, Section 3.1]
, replacing
F
by a finite extension if necessary, for each integer
m>0
we get

(a)

a newform
f_{m}\in S_{k_{m}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
with
\mathbf{Q}(f_{m})\subset F,\ k_{m}>2
and
k_{m}\equiv 2\pmod{p-1}
and such that
a_{p}(f_{m})\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
;

(b)

an equality of ideals
(\mathcal{L}^{S}_{f},\mathfrak{p}^{m})=(\mathcal{L}^{S}_{f_{m}},\mathfrak{p}^{m})\subseteq\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}
;

(c)

\overline{\rho}_{f}\cong\overline{\rho}_{f_{m}}
is reducible. In particular, the characters appearing in their semisimplifications are the same (both globally and locally);

(d)

\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f}(f_{m})
is a torsion
\Lambda
-module and Char
(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{fm})\Lambda^{\operatorname{nr}}=(\mathcal{L}^{S}_{f_{m}})\subseteq\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}
;

(e)

\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m},{\operatorname{free}}}
has no nonzero finite
\Lambda
-submodules, so Fitt
(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m},{\operatorname{free}}})={\operatorname{Char}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m},{\operatorname{free}}})
. Here
\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m},{\operatorname{free}}}
denotes the
\Lambda
-free part of
\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m}}
and Fitt denotes the
\Lambda
-Fitting ideal. The same is true for
f
.

(a) and (c) follow from Hida theory. Similarly as in
op. cit.
, (b) comes from a
2
-variable
p
-adic
L
-function (adding a ‘weight’ variable) for the Hida families constructed in
[
Cas20
, Theorem 2.11]
which works for Eisenstein primes. (d) follows from
theorem

3.0.8
(IMC2) for good ordinary forms
f_{m}
in the previous sections, the surjectivity of global-to-local maps (
remark

1.2.3
and
remark

1.4.2
) and the equality of local factors appearing in the definition of the imprimitive Selmer groups and
p
-adic
L
-functions. Note that by
theorem

3.0.6
we only know the Main Conjectures for a subfamily with weights
k_{m}\equiv 2\pmod{4}
. But such subfamily is always infinite so the following limiting argument applies. (e) comes from basic properties of Fitting ideals.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

The
\mu
-invariants of
\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f}
and
\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m}}
are
0
.

Proof.

This is essentially proved in
theorem

1.4.1
since the proof only relies on the extension (
5.1
). Note that the characters can be arbitrary.
∎

By (c) above, for every
f_{m}
, we also have an extension

0\to\mathbf{F}(\varphi)\to\overline{\rho}_{f_{m}}\to\mathbf{F}(\psi)\to 0.

theorem

1.5.1
then implies that
\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f})=\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m}})
. On the other hand, since the
\mu
-invariants are
0
,
\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f})
(resp.
\lambda(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m}})
) determines
\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f,{\operatorname{free}}}/\mathfrak{p}^{m}
(resp.
\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m},{\operatorname{free}}}/\mathfrak{p}^{m}
). Thus there is an equality of ideals

({\operatorname{Fitt}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f,{\operatorname{free}}}),\mathfrak{p}^{m})=({\operatorname{Fitt}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m},{\operatorname{free}}}),\mathfrak{p}^{m}).

Then following the arguments in
[
Ski14
, Section 3.1]
, we have the following result.

Lemma 5.1.2
.

{\operatorname{Char}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f})\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}=(\mathcal{L}^{S}_{f})\subseteq\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle({\operatorname{Char}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f})\Lambda^{\operatorname{nr}},\mathfrak{p}^{m})

\displaystyle=({\operatorname{Char}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f,{\operatorname{free}}})\Lambda^{\operatorname{nr}},\mathfrak{p}^{m})

\displaystyle=({\operatorname{Fitt}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f,{\operatorname{free}}})\Lambda^{\operatorname{nr}},\mathfrak{p}^{m})

\displaystyle=({\operatorname{Fitt}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m},{\operatorname{free}}})\Lambda^{\operatorname{nr}},\mathfrak{p}^{m})

\displaystyle=({\operatorname{Char}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m},{\operatorname{free}}})\Lambda^{\operatorname{nr}},\mathfrak{p}^{m})

\displaystyle=({\operatorname{Char}}(\mathfrak{X}^{S}_{f_{m}})\Lambda^{\operatorname{nr}},\mathfrak{p}^{m})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}^{S}_{f_{m}},\mathfrak{p}^{m})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}^{S}_{f},\mathfrak{p}^{m}).

The result then follows from taking limit with respect to
m
as in
[
Ski14
, section 3.1]
.
∎

Theorem 5.1.3
.

{\operatorname{Char}}(\mathfrak{X}_{f})\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}=(\mathcal{L}_{f})\subseteq\Lambda^{{\operatorname{nr}}}.

Proof.

Similar to the good reduction case, this result follows from the comparison between primitive and imprimitive Selmer groups (see
theorem

1.5.1
, whose proof still works in the multiplicative reduction setting) and the comparison between primitive and imprimitive
p
-adic
L
-functions (see
theorem

2.2.2
). We mention that the same arguments as in
[
Kri16
, Prop. 37]
(which has an assumption
p\nmid N
) still work since Kriz’s computation of the congruence is with the
p
-depletion which is identified with the
((p^{2})^{(0)})
-stabilization, which allows
p
in the level.
∎

5.2.
A
p
-converse theorem

Similar as in the good reduction case, we also obtain a
p
-converse theorem for multiplicative reduction. Via an explicit reciprocity law, one can argue as in
[
Cas24
]
to show that the BDP Main Conjecture proved in the last section is equivalent to a Heegner Point Main Conjecture which would then yield a
p
-converse theorem.

As in
[
Cas24
]
, one can consider a Heegner class
\mathbf{z}_{\infty}^{*}\in\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})
which is either a standard
\Lambda
-adic Heegner class
\mathbf{z}_{\infty}
(denoted as
\kappa_{\infty}
by us in section
3
) in the non-split multiplicative case, or a derived Heegner class
\mathbf{z}_{\infty}^{\prime}
defined by the relation
\mathbf{z}_{\infty}=(\gamma-1)\mathbf{z}_{\infty}^{\prime}
in the split multiplicative case. Here
\gamma
is a topological generator of
{\operatorname{Gal}}(K_{\infty}/K)
, and this
(\gamma-1)
factor reflects a trivial zero of the
p
-adic
L
-function. We have the following Heegner Point Main Conjecture for multiplicative primes (the definitions are the same as those for good ordinary primes).

Theorem 5.2.1
.

Let
f\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
be a newform of weight
2
and assume that
p\mid\mid N
is an Eisenstein prime for
f
. Then both
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})
and
\mathcal{X}=\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,M_{f})^{\vee}
have
\Lambda
-rank one, and the equality

{\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{X}_{{\operatorname{tors}}})={\operatorname{Char}}_{\Lambda}(\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}(K,\mathbf{T})/\Lambda\mathbf{z}_{\infty}^{*})^{2}

holds.

Proof.

This follows from the explicit reciprocity law
[
Cas24
, Theorem 2.2, Corollary 2.3]
for both split and non-split cases, which can be applied to prove the equivalence of this Heegner Point Main Conjecture to
theorem

5.1.3
. In showing the equivalence using
[
Cas24
, Proposition 3.2]
(also
[
BCK21
, Theorem 5.1]
), we could again first assume
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,\overline{\rho}_{f})=0
(see also
proposition

1.3.3
) and deduce the general case using a combination of Ribet’s lemma and the invariance of the Main Conjectures.

It should be mentioned that in this situation we do not need to consider the
t>0
case from
theorem

3.0.6
, since that is only needed to get one divisibility from the Kolyvagin system argument but not needed for the equivalence.
∎

Corollary 5.2.2
.

Let
A/\mathbf{Q}
be a simple RM abelian variety associated to a newform
f
and
\mathfrak{P}\mid p>2
a prime ideal of its endomorphism ring
\mathcal{O}={\operatorname{End}}(A)
of multiplicative reduction such that
\rho_{A,\mathfrak{P}}
is reducible.

Then

{\operatorname{corank}}_{\mathcal{O}}{\operatorname{Sel}}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\infty}}(A/\mathbf{Q})=r\in\{0,1\}\implies{\operatorname{rk}}_{\mathcal{O}}A(\mathbf{Q})=r_{\operatorname{an}}(f)=r,

and
\Sha(A/\mathbf{Q})[\mathfrak{P}^{\infty}]
is finite.

Proof.

This follows from the proof of
[
Cas24
, Theorem 1.1]
for multiplicative primes and a control theorem of Greenberg (see
[
Cas24
, Theorem 3.4]
), where the appeal to Theorem 1.3 from
loc. cit.
is replaced by
theorem

5.2.1
. Note that their arguments also work in the Eisenstein case.
∎

Appendix A
A Kolyvagin system argument

As in
[
How04
]
, by a coefficient ring
R
we mean a complete, Noetherian, local ring with finite residue field of characteristic
p
. If
L
is a perfect field, we denote by
\mathbf{Mod}_{R,L}
the category of finitely generated
R
-modules equipped with continuous, linear actions of
G_{L}
, assumed to be unramified outside of a finite set of primes in the case where
L
is a global field.

In this subsection,
R^{(k)}
is assumed to be an Artinian principal idea ring of length
k
. Denote its residue field by
\mathbf{F}
. Let
T^{(k)}
be an object of
\mathbf{Mod}_{R^{(k)},G_{K}}
. Fix a generator
\pi^{(k)}
of the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}^{(k)}
of
R^{(k)}
. We will ignore the indexes and simply write
\pi
and
\mathfrak{m}
when the
k
are clear from the contexts. Let
T^{(k)*}={\operatorname{Hom}}(T^{(k)},R(1))
and fix a Selmer structure
\mathcal{F}
on
T^{(k)}
. Let
\mathcal{F}^{*}
denote the dual Selmer structure on
T^{(k)*}
. In all what follows we assume that
(T^{(k)},\mathcal{F})
and
(T^{(k)*},\mathcal{F}^{*})
satisfy the hypotheses
\text{{H}}.0
and
\text{{H}}.3
–
\text{{H}}.5
in
op. cit.
§ 1.3. In particular, we do
not
assume
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,\overline{\rho}_{f})=0
(assumption (h1) in
[
CGLS22
, § 3.2]
), and
\text{{H}}.2
is not necessary for our purpose.

The main result of this appendix is
theorem

A.1.1
, which follows from
lemma

A.1.4
and the arguments in
[
How04
]
.

We will make the following assumption about stabilizing
\text{{H}}^{0}
groups throughout this section:

Assumption A.0.1
.

There is an absolute constant
N
such that
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)})\cong R/\pi^{k}
when
k\leq N
, and
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)})\cong R/\pi^{N}
for all
k>N
.

Note that by a version of
proposition

1.3.4
(i) in the case where
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,\overline{\rho}_{f})\neq 0
,
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,V_{f}/T_{f})
is still finite (since
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,V_{f})=0
because
\rho_{f}
is irreducible), this assumption is satisfied when
T^{(k)}=T_{f}/\pi^{k}T_{f}
where
\pi
is the uniformizer of
R
. For the exposition, we often denote
\text{{H}}^{i}(K,-)
by
\text{{H}}^{i}(-)
.

Following
[
How04
, Lemma 1.3.3]
and
[
MR04
, Lemma 3.5.4]
, for
i\leq k
, we would like to compare
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)})
and
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{i}])
with
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{i}]
. Note that the first two groups are isomorphic (see
[
How04
, Remark 1.1.4]
), we will use
T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)}
and
T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{i}]
interchangeably without further explanation in cohomology groups. In
[
How04
]
, the vanishing of several
\text{{H}}^{0}
groups forces the natural maps between the above groups to be isomorphisms. Since we allow non-trivial
\text{{H}}^{0}
groups, we need to study the kernels and cokernels more carefully.

Lemma A.0.2
.

Let
k\geq i
. The injection
\pi^{k-i}\colon T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)}\hookrightarrow T^{(k)}
induces morphisms

\displaystyle[\pi^{k-i}]\colon

\displaystyle(\text{{H}}^{1}(K,T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{i}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}})\ \text{{H}}^{1}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)})\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K,T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{i}]

\displaystyle[\pi^{k-i}]\colon

\displaystyle(\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{i}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}})\ \text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)})\to\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{i}]

with finite kernel
K^{(i)}
and cokernel
C^{(i)}
and finite kernel
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}
and cokernel
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}
, respectively.

One has
|K^{(i)}|=|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}|=|C^{(i)}|=\frac{|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)})||\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{k-i}T^{(k)})|}{|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)})|}\geq|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(k)}|
.

Proof.

We want to point it out that this is essentially already proved in
[
MR04
, Lemma 3.5.4]
. Cohomology of the following two exact sequences

(i)

0\to T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)}\xrightarrow{\pi^{k-i}}T^{(k)}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{k-i}T^{(k)}\to 0

(ii)

0\to T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{k-i}T^{(k)}\xrightarrow{\pi^{i}}T^{(k)}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)}\to 0

gives
K^{(i)}\simeq\frac{\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{k-i}T^{(k)})}{p_{1}(\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}))}
and
C^{(i)}\simeq\frac{\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)})}{p_{2}(\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}))}
. (In particular, they are finite, so we can speak about their order.) But
|p_{1}(\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}))|=\frac{|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k)})|}{|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)})|}
and
|p_{2}(\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}))|=\frac{|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k)})|}{|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{k-i}T^{(k)})|}
, so
|K^{(i)}|=|C^{(i)}|=\frac{|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}T^{(k)})||\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{k-i}T^{(k)})|}{|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)})|}
.

To pass to Selmer groups, consider the following diagram (omitting
K
in the cohomology groups) with the “map defining the Selmer groups” the global-to-local map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{i}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{i}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\text{map\ defining\ }\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{i}]))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{i}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{i}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\text{map\ defining\ }\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}))[\mathfrak{m}^{i}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

What is proved in
[
MR04
, Lemma 3.5.4]
is equivalent to
\ker(f)=0
. Thus by the snake lemma
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}=K^{(i)}
and
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}\hookrightarrow C^{(i)}
. The claim follows.
∎

Remark A.0.3
.

In the situations considered by the aforementioned authors (i.e., if (h1) holds), we have
K^{(i)}=C^{(i)}=0
. Then it easily follows that
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}=C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}=0
, too. In our situation, three of them are equal and can be described explicitly, but
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}
is only bounded from above. It seems very hard to determine
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}
completely, but as we will see eventually, it is enough to control its growth using induction.

A.1.
Proof of a structure theorem of finite level Selmer groups

To ease notation, let
(T,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
denote the Selmer triple
(T_{\alpha},\mathcal{F}_{{\operatorname{ord}}},\mathcal{L}_{f})
, and let
\rho=\rho_{\alpha}
. For any
k\geq 0
, let

R^{(k)}=R/\mathfrak{m}^{k}R,\ T^{(k)}=T/\mathfrak{m}^{k}T,\ \mathcal{L}^{(k)}=\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}:I_{\ell}\subset p^{k}\mathbf{Z}_{p}\},

and let
\mathcal{N}^{(k)}
be the set of square-free products of primes
\ell\in\mathcal{L}^{(k)}
.

Recall that we have the self-duality
T\simeq T^{*}
and
(T^{(k)})^{*}\simeq T^{(k)}
.

Theorem A.1.1
.

For every
n\in\mathcal{N}^{(k)}
where
k\gg 0
, there is an
R^{(k)}
-module
M^{(k)}(n)
and an integer
\varepsilon
such that there is a pseudo-isomorphism with kernel and cokernel bounded independent of
k
(which can be computed explicitly):

(A.1)

\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,T^{(k)})\sim(R^{(k)})^{\varepsilon}\oplus M^{(k)}(n)\oplus M^{(k)}(n).

Moreover,
\varepsilon
can be taken to be
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
and is independent of all
k
for which
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)})
is not free, and
n
.

Proof.

We follow the ideas in
[
How04
, sections 1.4–1.5]
. As in Theorem 1.4.2 in
op. cit.
, we still define

V_{s}=\frac{\mathcal{H}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}{\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{H}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]},\quad W_{s}=\frac{\mathcal{H}[\mathfrak{m}]}{\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{H}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]},

where
\mathcal{H}=\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)})
is a finitely generated
R^{(k)}
-module and
1\leq s\leq k
.

We also define

P_{s}=\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{s}T^{(k)})}{{\operatorname{proj}}(\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}T^{(k)}))},\quad Q_{s}=\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,(T^{(k)})^{*}[\mathfrak{m}])}{\pi^{s}\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,(T^{(k)})^{*}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}.

Here
{\operatorname{proj}}
denotes the canonical projection and
Q_{s}
can be identified with
\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])}{\pi^{s}\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
by self-duality.
To further ease notation, let
\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{i}])
denote
\text{{H}}_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}(K,T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{i}])
. We may then identity
P_{s}
with
\frac{\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}{\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
and rewrite
Q_{s}
as
\frac{\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}])}{\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
. Note that these isomorphisms may not be canonical, but as will be clear momentarily, we only care about the sizes of these groups.

The Cassels–Tate pairing (see
[
How04
, Proposition 1.4.1, Theorem 1.4.2]
) gives a nondegenerate pairing of finite dimensional
R^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}
-vector spaces

(\ ,\ )_{s,1}\colon P_{s}\times Q_{s}\to R^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}].

However, in our case,
P_{s}
(resp.
Q_{s}
) is not necessarily isomorphic to
V_{s}
(resp.
W_{s}
). Moreover,
K_{s}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ker(P_{s}\to Q_{s})
is not the same as
P_{s-1}
.
What the arguments in
[
How04
, Theorem 1.4.2]
show for our case is that for each
s<k
,
\frac{P_{s}}{K_{s}}
is even dimensional as
R^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}
-vector spaces. We claim that
\frac{V_{s}}{V_{s-1}}
(which is no longer identified with
\frac{P_{s}}{K_{s}}
) is also even dimensional for all but maybe two
s
’s bounded (independent of
k
). This will be proved in the following series of lemmas, where we compare the dimensions of
\frac{P_{s}}{K_{s}}
and
\frac{V_{s}}{V_{s-1}}
using direct computations.

More precisely, we will assume
\mathcal{H}
is not free as an
R^{(k)}
-module (otherwise it automatically has the desired structure (
A.1
) with
M^{(k)}(n)=0
and
\varepsilon=1
), and show that
\frac{V_{s}}{V_{s-1}}
is even dimensional for all
1\leq s<k
except at
N
and some
1\leq N_{0}\leq N+1
independent of
s
to be defined later, where
N
is given as in
A.0.1
. When
N=N_{0}
, all
\frac{V_{s}}{V_{s-1}}
will be even. See
lemma

A.1.4
for the precise statement. Then by the structure theorem for finitely generated
R^{(k)}
-modules, there is a finitely generated
R^{(k)}
-module
M^{(k)}
such that

\mathcal{H}\cong(R^{(k)})^{\varepsilon}\oplus M^{(k)}\oplus M^{(k)}\oplus E^{(k)},

where the “error term”
E^{(k)}=E^{(k_{1})}\oplus E^{(k_{2})}
is a direct sum of two (possibly isomorphic) cyclic
R^{(k)}
-modules of lengths between
1
and
N+1
. Since this proof (and those of the following lemmas) still works if we replace
\mathcal{F}
by any
\mathcal{F}(n)
, we also get

\text{{H}}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}^{1}(K,T^{(k)})\cong(R^{(k)})^{\varepsilon_{k}}\oplus M^{(k)}(n)\oplus M^{(k)}(n)\oplus E^{(k)}(n)

for some
R^{(k)}
-module
M^{(k)}
where
E^{(k)}(n)
again consists of two cyclic summands of lengths between
1
and
N+1
.

That
\varepsilon
is independent of
n\in\mathcal{N}
is due to the “parity lemma” of
[
How04
, Lemma 1.5.3]
, which says
\rho(n)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\text{dim}_{R^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}}\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,\bar{T})\pmod{2}
is independent of
n\in\mathcal{N}
. The same argument can be modified to work in our situation. Indeed, we will see that
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,\bar{T})
and
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]
will have the same size if
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,T^{(k)})
is not free, and differ by
\oplus\mathbf{F}
otherwise. Then if
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,T^{(k)})
is not free, we have

\varepsilon+2\text{dim}_{R^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}}M(n)^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}]+E^{(k)}(n)[\mathfrak{m}]=\text{dim}_{R^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}}\text{{H}}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}^{1}(K,T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]=\rho(n),

so
\varepsilon\equiv\rho(n)\pmod{2}
, and if
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,T^{(k)})
is free,
\varepsilon\equiv\rho(n)+1\pmod{2}
.
∎

Lemma A.1.2
.

For a fixed
k\gg 0
and
s<k
,

K_{s}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ker(P_{s}\to Q_{s})\simeq\frac{\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}]}{\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}.

Proof.

Recall that
\ker(P_{s}\to Q_{s})
fits in the exact sequence

0\to\ker(P_{s}\to Q_{s})\to\frac{\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}{\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}}\frac{\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}])}{\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])},

so we can write
\ker(P_{s}\to Q_{s})=\frac{(\mathfrak{m}^{s-1})^{-1}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]))\cap\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}{\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
.

We claim that the (well-defined) natural map

\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}]\to\frac{(\mathfrak{m}^{s-1})^{-1}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]))\cap\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}{\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}

induced by quotient after inclusion is surjective.

Indeed, let
c\in(\mathfrak{m}^{s-1})^{-1}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]))\cap\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])
, then
\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}c=\mathfrak{m}^{s}c_{0}
for some
c_{0}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])
. Let
a=\mathfrak{m}c_{0}\in\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])\subset\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])
, then
\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}(c-a)=0
so
c-a\in\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}]
and
c-a
maps to the class of
c
.

We now compute the kernel of the above map. By definition, the kernel is

\displaystyle\{\text{classes\ of\ }c\in\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]):\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}c=0,c=\mathfrak{m}a,a\in\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\text{classes\ of\ c}\in\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]):\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}c=0,c=\mathfrak{m}a,\mathfrak{m}^{s}a=0,a\in\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\text{classes\ of\ c}\in\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]):\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}c=0,c=\mathfrak{m}a,a\in\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]),

hence the lemma.
∎

Lemma A.1.3
.

For a fixed
k\gg 0
and all
s<k
,

\frac{|\frac{V_{s}}{V_{s-1}}|}{|\frac{P_{s}}{K_{s}}|}=(|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|)^{-2}\times\frac{|K_{s}^{s+1}|}{|K_{s-1}^{s}|}\times\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|\times\Bigg(\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}|}/|C_{s-1}^{s}|\Bigg)^{-1}\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|,

where
K_{s}^{s+1},C_{s}^{s+1}
(and similarly
K_{s-1}^{s},C_{s-1}^{s}
) are the kernel and cokernel of the morphism

\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(s+1)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])\to\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(s+1)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s}].

Proof.

Denote by
N(P_{s}):=\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])
and
D(P_{s}):=\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])
the numerator and denominator of
P_{s}
. Similarly define
N(K_{s}),D(K_{s}),N(V_{s-1}),D(V_{s-1}),N(V_{s}),D(V_{s})
. We will study the following
4
maps separately.

N(P_{s})\to N(V_{s})
:
This is the map

0\to K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}\to\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])\xrightarrow{N_{P\to V}}\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]\to C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}\to 0.

So
\frac{|N(P_{s})|}{|N(V_{s})|}=\frac{|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}
.

D(P_{s})\to D(V_{s})
:
This is the map

\mathfrak{m}\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])\xrightarrow{D_{P\to V}}\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]).

To study the kernel and cokernel of this map, we study the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(D_{P\to V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])[\mathfrak{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}_{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}_{(2)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}{D_{P\to V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}_{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{P\to V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}_{(2)}}

From the snake lemma, we see that
\frac{|D(P_{s})|}{|D(V_{s})|}=\frac{|\ker(D_{P\to V})|}{|{\operatorname{coker}}(D_{P\to V})|}=\frac{|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}\frac{|\bar{C}|}{|\bar{K}|}
.

N(K_{s})\to N(V_{s-1})
:
This is the map

\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}]\xrightarrow{N_{K\to V}}\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}],

To study the kernel and cokernel of this map, we need to consider the following diagram with
2
familiar maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{s-1}^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}][\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{s-1}^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{K\to V}}

Comparing the dimensions, we see that
\frac{|N(K_{s})|}{|N(V_{s-1})|}=\frac{|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|K_{s-1}^{s}|}\frac{|C_{s-1}^{s}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}
.

D(K_{s})\to D(V_{s-1})
:
This is the map

\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])\xrightarrow{D_{K\to V}}\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]).

To study the kernel and cokernel of this map, we study the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(D_{K\to V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])[\mathfrak{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}_{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T)[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}][\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}_{(2)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{coker}}{D_{K\to V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}_{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{K\to V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}_{(2)}}

Note that the first column also appeared in the second case and the middle column is already studied in the previous case (except we had
s-1
in place of
s
): we have
\frac{|\hat{K}|}{|\hat{C}|}=\frac{|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|K_{s}^{s+1}|}\frac{|C_{s}^{s+1}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}
.

Now the snake lemma tells us that
\frac{|D(K_{s})|}{|D(V_{s-1})|}=\frac{|\ker(D_{K\to V})|}{|{\operatorname{coker}}(D_{K\to V})|}=\frac{|\hat{K}|}{|\hat{C}|}\frac{|\bar{C}|}{|\bar{K}|}=\frac{|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|K_{s}^{s+1}|}\frac{|C_{s}^{s+1}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}\frac{|\bar{C}|}{|\bar{K}|}
.

Now

\displaystyle\frac{|\frac{V_{s}}{V_{s-1}}|}{|\frac{P_{s}}{K_{s}}|}=

\displaystyle\frac{|N(V_{s})|}{|N(P_{s})|}\frac{|D(V_{s-1})|}{|D(K_{s})|}\frac{|D(P_{s})|}{|D(V_{s})|}\frac{|N(K_{s})|}{|N(V_{s-1})|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}\frac{|K_{s}^{s+1}|}{|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{s}^{s+1}|}\frac{|\bar{K}|}{|\bar{C}|}\frac{|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}\frac{|\bar{C}|}{|\bar{K}|}\frac{|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|K_{s-1}^{s}|}\frac{|C_{s-1}^{s}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|)^{-2}\times\frac{|K_{s}^{s+1}|}{|K_{s-1}^{s}|}\times\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|\times(\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}|}/|C_{s-1}^{s}|)^{-1}\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|.

∎

Despite that we do not have full understanding of each
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}
, we are able to determine the parity of the dimension of the right hand side by comparing these cokernels inductively, provided that
k
is sufficiently large.

Lemma A.1.4
.

For a fixed
k\gg 0
, if
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{(k)})
is not
R^{(k)}
-free, then there is an
N_{0}
with
1\leq N_{0}\leq N+1
such that
\frac{|K_{s}^{s+1}|}{|K_{s-1}^{s}|}\times\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|\times\Bigg(\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}|}/|C_{s-1}^{s}|\Bigg)^{-1}\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|
is an even power of
|\mathbf{F}|
when
s\neq N_{0},N
. When
s=N_{0}
or
N
when
N\neq N_{0}
, the product is an odd power of
|\mathbf{F}|
, and when
s=N_{0}=N
, the product is an even power. Consequently, by
lemma

A.1.3
,
\frac{V_{s}}{V_{s-1}}
is even-dimensional for all
s\neq N_{0},N
. When
N\neq N_{0}
, it is odd-dimensional for
s=N,N_{0}
. When
N=N_{0}
, it is even-dimensional for
s=N=N_{0}
and hence for all
1\leq s<k
.

Proof.

Taking cohomology of the short exact sequence

0\to T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]\to T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{m}^{s}}T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}]\to 0

yields an exact sequence (omitting
K
from the cohomology groups)

0\to\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])/\mathfrak{m}^{s}\to\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])\to\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])\to\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])\to\ldots

Note that
T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]\simeq T^{(k)}/\mathfrak{m}^{s}\simeq T^{(s)}/\mathfrak{m}^{s}\simeq T^{(s)}
, we have
|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])/\mathfrak{m}^{s}|=\frac{|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])||\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])|}{|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])|}=\frac{|\text{{H}}^{0}(\bar{T})||\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(s)})|}{|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(s+1)})|}
.

We assume
k>2N+1
and we consider several cases depending on how big
s
is and how close it is to
k
. When
1\leq s<N
, the above quotient is trivial by computing dimensions. When
s\geq N
,
|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(s)})|=|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(s+1)})|
so the above quotient is (non-canonically)
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,\bar{T})\cong\mathbf{F}
.

Now consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(s)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{|\text{{H}}^{0}(\bar{T})||\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(s)})|}{|\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(s+1)})|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{(s)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{s+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{1}}

When
1\leq s<N
(so
k-s>N
), by
lemma

A.0.2
and
A.0.1
,
|K^{(s)}|=|C^{(s)}|=\frac{|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(s)})||\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k-s)})|}{|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)})|}=|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(s)})|=|\mathbf{F}|^{s}
and
|K^{(s+1)}|=|C^{(s+1)}|=|\mathbf{F}|^{s+1}
. Note also that
K^{(1)}=C^{(1)}=\mathbf{F}
.

To pass to the Selmer groups, we have the following diagram (noting
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}=K^{(i)}
for all
i
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/\pi^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/\pi^{s+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(1)}(\subset C^{(1)}\simeq\mathbf{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}}

By the structure theorem of finitely generated
R^{(k)}
-modules, the map
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}
is surjective if and only if
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})
has a summand with height less than or equal to
s
. Let
N_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\min\{s\leq k:\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}\text{is not surjective}\}
. Then it is clear that if
s\geq N_{0}
,
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}
will also be non-surjective.

Note that when
N_{0}=k
,
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})
must be a free
R^{(k)}
-module. We claim that if
N_{0}\neq k
, then
N_{0}\leq N+1
. Namely, the lowest height of all non-free summands of
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})
must have height less than or equal to
N
. In other words, assuming
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})
is not a free
R^{(k)}
-module, as soon as
s\geq N+1
,
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}
will never be surjective.

Assume by contradiction that
N_{0}>N+1
. Then for all
1\leq s\leq N+1
,
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}
is surjective. Consider the following diagram when
s<N

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/\pi^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/\pi^{s+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{s}(\subset\mathbf{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{s}(\subset C^{(1)}\simeq\mathbf{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}}

Since
K_{s}
contains
{\operatorname{coker}}(R/\pi^{s}\to R/\pi^{s+1})
,
K_{s}
is at least
\mathbf{F}
, so
K_{s}=\mathbf{F}
. Similarly, when
s=N
or
N+1
, the first row becomes

0\to\text{{H}}^{0}(\bar{T})\cong\mathbf{F}\to\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s)})\cong R/\pi^{N}\to\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s-1)})\cong R/\pi^{N}\to K_{s},

and
K_{s}
must be
\mathbf{F}
. Therefore the snake lemma shows that
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}\to C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}
is injective with cokernel
C_{s}
. We consider
2
subcases.

Case I:
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
is never surjective for
1\leq s\leq N+1
.
In this case, consider the rightmost vertical map
f^{1^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{F}}
. If we replace its domain
{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)})
by
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])
which is strictly bigger in dimension, since the codomain is fixed and the kernel cannot be any bigger, its cokernel must change from
C_{s}
to something strictly smaller. Therefore
C_{s}\neq 0
and
C_{s}=\mathbf{F}
. Then for all
1\leq s\leq N+1
, the dimension of
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}
increases whenever
s
increases, so
|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(N+2)}|\geq|\mathbf{F}^{N+1}|>|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(N+2)}|
. But
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}\subset K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}
by
lemma

A.0.2
, contradiction.

Case II:
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
is surjective for some
1\leq s\leq N+1
.
In this case, the rightmost vertical map
f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1^{\prime}}
agrees with the map
f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}\colon\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])\to\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]
and is independent of
s
. Therefore if for one such
s
the cokernel of
f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1^{\prime}}
(so also that of
f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
) is
\mathbf{F}
, then the cokernel is
\mathbf{F}
for all such
s
. Therefore
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}
also has one more dimension than
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}
when
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
is surjective. But for other
s
, the same is true as in Case I, so again
|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(N+2)}|
would be too big. Thus the cokernel of
f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
must be
0
. This in turn means all
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}
must be surjective because otherwise if we replace
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]
by
{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)})
, the cokernel of
f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
must be smaller (since
f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
has fixed domain and kernel), which is impossible. But in this case
N_{0}=k
and
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})
is free.

So far we showed that either
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})
is free or it has a summand with height lower than
N+1
, and as a consequence we may assume
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}
is not surjective for all
s>N
which is independent of
k
. Moreover, in this case
f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
will have kernel and cokernel both equal to
\mathbf{F}
, so in what follows, we will keep this assumption.

We first study the dimensions appearing in this lemma when
k>s>N
. By
lemma

A.0.2
and
A.0.1
,
|K^{(s)}|=|C^{(s)}|=\frac{|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(s)})||\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k-s)}|}{|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k)})|}=|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k-s)})|
and
|K^{(s+1)}|=|C^{(s+1)}|=|\text{{H}}^{0}(K,T^{(k-s-1)})|
. Note also that
K^{(1)}=C^{(1)}=\mathbf{F}
.

To pass to the Selmer groups, we have the following diagram(noting
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}=K^{(i)}
for all
i
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(\bar{T})=\mathbf{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s-1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(1)}(\subset C^{(1)}\simeq\mathbf{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}}

To apply snake lemma, we consider the following diagram where the first two rows may or may not be changed depending on whether
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
is surjective:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s-1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\subset\mathbf{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(1)}(\subset C^{(1)}\simeq\mathbf{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}}

In the rightmost column, if we replace
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]
by
{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)})
which is strictly smaller, the only possibility is
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(1)}
gets strictly smaller because the domain and kernel of
f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1^{\prime}}
are determined by other columns(by snake lemma) which do not change. This in turn means
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(1)}
must be
\mathbf{F}
to begin with, so if we do consider
{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)})
we get the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s-1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\subset\mathbf{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s+1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}}

When
k=s+1
, we have a special diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}(\subset\mathbf{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(s+1)}[\mathfrak{m}^{s}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(s+1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(s+1)})[\mathfrak{m}^{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(s+1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2^{\prime})})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{s}^{s+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{s^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{F}}^{1^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2^{\prime})}}

from which we see
C_{s}^{s+1}=K^{\prime}
by the snake lemma.

Our aim is to compare
|C_{s}^{s+1}|
with
|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}/C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|=|K|
.
Depending on whether
k-s\leq N
there are
2
different possibilities:

Case i:
s>N
and
k-s\leq N
.
In this case
\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s)})\cong R/\pi^{k-s}
and
\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s-1)})\cong R/\pi^{k-s-1}
so from snake lemma we get an exact sequence

0\to K\to C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}\to C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}\to 0.

Notice that the two
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
in the two diagrams are indeed the same map because the rows are identical, since
T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}^{i}]\simeq T^{(i)}
for any
i\leq k
. Now if
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
is surjective, then
K=K^{\prime}=K^{(1)}=\mathbf{F}
. If if
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
is not surjective, then in the last column if we replace
{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)})
by
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])(=\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(s+1)}[\mathfrak{m}]))
which is strictly bigger, it must be that
K
(and
K^{\prime}
) becomes strictly bigger because the codomain is fixed and the cokernel cannot be smaller. This in turn means
K=K^{\prime}=0
.

Thus in case i, we always have
K=K^{\prime}
and
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|=0
. Moreover, in case i,
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}\cong R/\pi^{n-s+1}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}\cong R/\pi^{n-s-1}
so
|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}||K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|
is an even power of
\mathbf{F}
.

Case ii:
s>N
and
k-s>N
.
In this case
\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s)})=\text{{H}}^{0}(T^{(k-s-1)})\cong R/\pi^{N}
, so we have an exact row

0\to\mathbf{F}\to R/\pi^{N}\to R/\pi^{N}\ \to K(\subset\mathbf{F}),

which forces
K=\mathbf{F}
. Furthermore, by the snake lemma this means
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}=C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}
. This also implies
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
is surjective, so
C_{s}^{s+1}=K^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}
.

Thus in case ii,
K=K^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}
and
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|=|\mathbf{F}|
.

Finally, note that
K_{s}^{s+1}=\mathbf{F}
for all
s\geq N
and
K_{s-1}^{s}=0
if
s<N
by
lemma

A.0.2
and
A.0.1
.

To sum up, when
k
is at least
2N+2
, as
s
grows from
N+2
(so
s-1>N
) to
k
,
k-s
is first greater than
N
. For such
s
,
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|=|\mathbf{F}|=\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}/|C_{s-1}^{s}|
, and
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}\cong R/\pi^{N}=K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}
; when
s=k-N
,
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|=0
and
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}/|C_{s-1}^{s}|\cong\mathbf{F}
, and
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}\cong R/\pi^{N}
,
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}\cong R/\pi^{N-1}
; when
s>k-N
,
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|=1=\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}/|C_{s-1}^{s}|
, and
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}\cong R/\pi^{k-s+1}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}\cong R/\pi^{k-s-1}
. In all cases,

\frac{|K_{s}^{s+1}|}{|K_{s-1}^{s}|}\times\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|\times\Bigg(\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}|}/|C_{s-1}^{s}|\Bigg)^{-1}\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|

is an even power of
|\mathbf{F}|
.

Now the cases left are when
s\leq N
. When
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}
is not surjective, then similarly as in Case II,
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
must be surjective. Then for such
s
,
C_{s}^{s+1}=|\mathbf{F}|
if
s=N
and
C_{s}^{s+1}=0
if
s<N
. Also
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}\simeq C_{\mathcal{F}}^{s+1}
, so
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|=|\mathbf{F}|^{-1}
if
s=N
and
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|=1
if
s<N
.

When
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}
is surjective, in fact,
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)}
must be surjective, too. Note that for
s\leq N
we have the following sequence

0\to\mathbf{F}\to{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)})\xrightarrow{f^{1^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{F}}}\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]\to C_{s}(\subset C^{(1)}\simeq\mathbf{F})\to 0.

If
\mathfrak{m}^{S}_{(1)}
is not surjective, then enlarging
{\operatorname{im}}(\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(1)})
would shrink
C_{s}
, yielding an exact sequence

0\to\mathbf{F}\to\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)}[\mathfrak{m}])\xrightarrow{f^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}}\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(T^{(k)})[\mathfrak{m}]\to 0,

which would mean
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(2)}
are always surjective, a contradiction.

So as long as
\mathfrak{m}^{s}_{(s)}
is surjective,
C_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
is increasing steadily. And if
s+1\leq N
, then
C_{s}^{s+1}=0
by
lemma

A.0.2
. So
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|=|\mathbf{F}|^{-1}
if
s<N
. When
s=N
, since
\mathfrak{m}^{N}_{(1)}
is surjective,
C_{N}^{(N+1)}=K^{\prime}\cong\mathbf{F}
. So
\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|=1
.

We summarize the behavior of the terms appearing in the lemma in the following table:

\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|

\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}/|C_{s-1}^{s}|

|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|

|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|

\frac{|K_{s}^{s+1}|}{|K_{s-1}^{s}|}

N<S<k-N

|\mathbf{F}|

(s-1>N):|\mathbf{F}|

|\mathbf{F}|^{N}

|\mathbf{F}|^{N}

1

(s-1=N\geq N_{0}):|\mathbf{F}|

(s-1=N<N_{0}):1

S=N<N_{0}

1

|\mathbf{F}|^{-1}

|\mathbf{F}|^{N}

|\mathbf{F}|^{N-1}

|\mathbf{F}|

S=N=N_{0}

|\mathbf{F}|^{-1}

|\mathbf{F}|^{-1}

|\mathbf{F}|^{N}

|\mathbf{F}|^{N-1}

|\mathbf{F}|

S=N>N_{0}

|\mathbf{F}|^{-1}

1

|\mathbf{F}|^{N}

|\mathbf{F}|^{N-1}

|\mathbf{F}|

N_{0}<S<N

1

|\mathbf{F}|^{s+1}

|\mathbf{F}|^{s-1}

1

S=N_{0}<N

1

|\mathbf{F}|^{-1}

|\mathbf{F}|^{s+1}

|\mathbf{F}|^{s-1}

1

S<N_{0}\leq N

|\mathbf{F}|^{-1}

|\mathbf{F}|^{-1}

|\mathbf{F}|^{s+1}

|\mathbf{F}|^{s-1}

1

We’re now able to determine the parity of the length of
\frac{|K_{s}^{s+1}|}{|K_{s-1}^{s}|}\times\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|}/|C_{s}^{s+1}|\times(\frac{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|}{|C_{\mathcal{F}}^{s}|}/|C_{s-1}^{s}|)^{-1}\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s-1)}|\times|K_{\mathcal{F}}^{(s+1)}|
as a power of
|\mathbf{F}|
via direct computation in different cases, depending on the relation between
N_{0}
and
N
.

Case 1:
N_{0}=N+1
.
In this case, when
s=N_{0}
and
s=N
we get an odd dimension, and even dimensions for all other
s\leq N+1
.

Case 2:
N_{0}=N
.
In this case, when get even dimensions for all
s\leq N+1
.

Case 3:
N_{0}=N-1
.
Same as in Case 1.

Case 4:
1<N_{0}<N-1
.
Same as in Case 1.

Case 5:
N_{0}=1
.
Same as in Case 1.
∎

Appendix B
An anticyclotomic control theorem

In this section we follow
[
JSW17
, section 3]
and
[
Gre99
, sections 3 and 4]
to prove an anticyclotomic control theorem with torsion, i.e., allowing
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,W)\neq 0
. We use the notations from
[
JSW17
]
and will also explain the difference between theirs and ours.

First, we make all the assumptions in
[
JSW17
, section 3.1]
except (irred
K
) and (HT) (which fails if the weight of
f
is higher than
2
). For our application to BSD formula in weight
2
we may still assume (HT), but we will also explain how to remove it for higher weights.

The first result we need from
op. cit.
is the following:

Proposition B.0.1
.

One has

\#\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}}(K,W)=\#\Sha_{{\mathrm{BK}\,}}(W/K)\cdot(\#\delta_{v})^{2},

where
\delta_{v}={\operatorname{coker}}\{\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{BK}\,}}}(K,T)\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{loc}}_{v}}\text{{H}}^{1}_{f}(K,W)/\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},T)_{{\operatorname{tors}}}\}
. In particular,
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}}(K,W)
has finite order.

Proof.

See
[
JSW17
, Proposition 3.2.1]
. Note that by Remark 3.2.2 in
op. cit.
, we do not need (irred
K
) to get
W^{*}=T^{\tau}
.

We mention that in the residually reducible setting,
\text{{H}}^{1}_{f}(K,T)
may no longer be a free
\mathcal{O}
-module. In particular, their
\delta_{v}
fits into the exact sequence

(B.1)

0\to\text{{H}}^{1}_{f}(K,T)_{{\operatorname{tors}}}\to\text{{H}}^{1}_{f}(K,T)\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{loc}}_{v}}\text{{H}}^{1}_{f}(K_{v},T)/\text{{H}}^{1}_{f}(K_{v},T)_{{\operatorname{tors}}}\to\delta_{v}\to 0,

where
\ker({\operatorname{loc}}_{v})
may not be
0
. This observation will be helpful for our application to modular abelian varieties in
section

B.3
.
∎

The main result of this appendix is the following anticyclotomic control theorem which allows torsion.

Theorem B.0.2

(Anticyclotomic control theorem)
.

The
\Lambda
-module
X^{\Sigma}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}(M_{f})
is
\Lambda
-torsion, and if
f^{\Sigma}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}(T)
is a generator of its characteristic
\Lambda
-ideal
{\operatorname{Char}}(X^{\Sigma}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}(M_{f}))
, then

\#\mathcal{O}/f^{\Sigma}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}(0)=\frac{\#\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}}(K,W)\cdot C^{\Sigma}(W)}{\#\text{{H}}^{0}(K,W)\cdot\#\text{{H}}^{0}(K,W)^{\vee}},

where

C^{\Sigma}(W)=\#\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{v},W)\cdot\#\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{\overline{v}},W)\cdot\prod_{w\in S_{p}\setminus\Sigma,w\ split}\#\text{{H}}^{1}_{{\operatorname{nr}}}(K_{w},W)\cdot\prod_{w\in\Sigma}\#\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},W).

We also define
\mathcal{P}={\displaystyle\prod_{w\in S\setminus\Sigma}\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},W)}{\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}}(K_{w},W)}}
and
\mathcal{G}={\operatorname{im}}\{\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{S}/K,W)\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{loc}}_{S}}\mathcal{P}\}.

We remark that their
S
and
\Sigma
are different from ours in section
1
. Their choice of
v,\overline{v}
is also different from ours. For our application, the
\Sigma
above would be taken as our
\Sigma-\{v,\overline{v},\infty\}
in the earlier sections and their
S
will be chosen to be our
\Sigma\cup\{v,\overline{v}\}
so that
X^{\Sigma}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}(M_{f})
is the dual of the imprimitive Greenberg Selmer group
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{S}_{Gr}}(K,M_{f})
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}=\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},W)}{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},W)_{{\mathrm{div}\,}}}\times\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},W)
.
In the rest of this appendix, we explain how to adapt the proofs in
[
JSW17
, section 3]
to our setting, following
[
Gre99
]
.

We begin with recalling
[
Gre99
, Lemma 4.2]
which says

(B.2)

\#\mathcal{O}/f^{\Sigma}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}(0)\sim\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}}{\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,M_{f})_{\Gamma}},

provided
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}
is finite. For now we assume this finiteness result so we have the above computation of
\#\mathcal{O}/f^{\Sigma}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}(0)
. We will first study the numerator.

B.1.
Computing
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}

As in
[
JSW17
, section 3.3.9]
, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{S}/K,W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{S}/K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(M_{f})^{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{loc}}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

where
\mathcal{P}(M_{f})={\displaystyle\prod_{w\in S\setminus\Sigma}\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},M_{f})}{\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}}(K_{w},M_{f})}}
. In
loc. cit.
, the first row is also exact on the right because
{\operatorname{loc}}_{S}
is surjective, but this surjectivity may fail for us as we do not assume (irred
K
). Therefore, we would need to work with a similar commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{S}/K,W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{S}/K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(M_{f})^{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{loc}}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where
\mathcal{G}(M_{f})={\operatorname{im}}\{\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{S}/K,M_{f})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{loc}}_{S}}\mathcal{P}(M_{f})\}
.

As in
loc. cit.
,
{\operatorname{coker}}(h)=0
since
\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{S}/K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}=\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{S}/K,M_{f})[\gamma-1]
and there is an exact sequence

0\to W\to M_{f}\xrightarrow{\gamma-1}M_{f}\to 0.

As
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}}(K,W)
is finite from
proposition

B.0.1
, snake lemma then yields

(B.3)

\frac{\#\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}}(K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}}{\#\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}}(K,W)}=\frac{\#{\operatorname{coker}}(s)}{\#\ker(s)}=\frac{\#\ker(g)}{\#\ker(h)},

where
\ker(h)=\text{{H}}^{0}(K,W)
which no longer vanishes in our case. We will see shortly that
\ker(g)
(and hence
{\operatorname{coker}}(s)
) is finite, which then gives the finiteness of
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,M_{f})^{\Gamma}
we needed. It remains to compute
\ker(g)
.

B.2.
Computing
\ker(g)

We first claim that
\mathcal{G}(M_{f})=\mathcal{P}(M_{f})
, or in other words, the map
\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{S}/K,M_{f})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{loc}}_{S}}\mathcal{P}(M_{f})\coloneq{\displaystyle\prod_{w\in S\setminus\Sigma}\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{w},M_{f})}{\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}}(K_{w},M_{f})}}=\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f})
is surjective. This is nothing but the global-to-local map defining the imprimitive Greenberg Selmer group, so it is surjective by
remark

1.4.2
.

We now prove a lemma.

Lemma B.2.1
.

\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})
is almost divisible, i.e., its Pontryagin dual has no non-trivial finite submodules.

Proof.

Similarly as in
proposition

1.2.5
, this follows from
[
Gre89
, Proposition 3, Proposition 5]
. Note that by
lemma

1.3.5
,
\text{{H}}^{2}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})
must be cotorsion. Thus it’s
0
and the result follows.
∎

From the proof of
lemma

1.3.5
, we see that the
\mathcal{O}
-corank of
\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},W)
is
2
. Since
{\operatorname{corank}}_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{P}={\operatorname{corank}}_{\mathcal{O}}\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},W)
, the same is true for
\mathcal{P}
. The arguments after Proposition 4.12 in
[
Gre99
]
then shows that
\text{{H}}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,M_{f})_{\Gamma}=0
. Now Theorem 4.7 in
op. cit.
gives the equation

\#\ker(g)=\#\ker(r)\cdot\#\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ac}\,}}^{\Sigma}}(K,M_{f})_{\Gamma}\cdot\#(\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{G}).

In our case the surjectivity of the map
(\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}(M_{f})^{\Gamma})=\big(\frac{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},W)}{\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{v},W)_{{\mathrm{div}\,}}}\times\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},W)\to\text{{H}}^{1}(K_{\overline{v}},M_{f})^{\Gamma}\big)
follows from the
p
-cohomological dimension of
G(K_{\infty,\overline{v}}/K_{\overline{v}})
being
1
.

Now by Cassels’ Theorem (Proposition 4.13 in
op. cit.
), since
\text{{H}}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{ac}}(K,W)
is finite by
proposition

B.0.1
and
\text{{H}}^{0}(K_{w},W)
is finite by
proposition

1.3.4
, we have
\mathcal{P}/\mathcal{G}\cong\text{{H}}^{0}(K,W)^{\vee}
.

Combining this result with (
B.2
), (
B.3
) and the computation of
\ker(r)
in
[
JSW17
, Proposition 3.3.7]
(the proof of case 3(b) is the only place where (HT) is used, but by
proposition

1.3.4
, we already have the finiteness of
M_{f}^{G_{K_{v}}}
so (HT) is no longer needed),
theorem

B.0.2
follows.

B.3.
Applications to newforms and modular abelian varieties
A_{f}

Most part of the computations are already done in
[
JSW17
, section 3.5]
.

As is already noted in
proposition

B.0.1
, in our residually reducible setting,
A_{f}(K)\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[f]}\mathcal{O}=\text{{H}}^{1}_{f}(K,T)
may no longer be a free
\mathcal{O}
-module, and we need to replace
A_{f}(K)
and
\text{{H}}^{1}_{f}(K,T)
in
loc. cit.
by
A_{f}(K)_{/{\operatorname{tors}}}
and
\text{{H}}^{1}_{f}(K,T)_{/{\operatorname{tors}}}
respectively. In particular, from the sequence (
B.1
), equation (3.5.a) in
op. cit.
becomes

\#\delta_{v}=\#[A_{f}(K_{v})_{/{\operatorname{tors}}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[f]\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}:A_{f}(K)_{/{\operatorname{tors}}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[f]}\mathcal{O}].

This means in our application to elliptic curves, the above index becomes
[E(K)_{/{\operatorname{tors}}}:\mathbf{Z}\cdot P]_{p}
, which does not include the torsion of
E(K)
. Indeed, as we will see immediately,
E(K)_{{\operatorname{tors}}}
has already been considered separately in
section

B.1
and
section

B.2
.

It remains to compute the torsion terms
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,W)
and
\text{{H}}^{0}(K,W)^{\vee}
. But these are identified with
(A_{f}(K)\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[f]}\mathcal{O})[\mathfrak{p}^{\infty}]
and its dual respectively, both having size
\#((A_{f}(K)\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[f]}\mathcal{O})_{{\operatorname{tors}}})_{\mathfrak{p}}
. When
A_{f}
is an elliptic curve,
\mathbf{Z}[f]=\mathbf{Z}
,
\mathcal{O}=\mathbf{Z}_{p}
and the size is
\#(E(K)\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p})[p^{\infty}]=\#(E(K)[p^{\infty}])=(\#E(K)_{{\operatorname{tors}}})_{p}
.
